The adaptive network is 42 → 16 → 2 with zero final-layer initialization; a
two-parameter constant adjustment is a matched control. AP reaches 0.4683, improving
the matched anchor by 0.0210 [.0015, .0419] and the constant adjustment by 0.0226
[.0036, .0432]. AUROC gains remain unresolved; recall falls and ECE rises relative to
the anchor. Threshold differences also prevent treating its hard decisions as identical to
historical F2. This is evidence for a particular exploratory function class, not
retrospective success of F6.
        Matched augmentation uses 50% clean, 25% pixel-noise and 25% sensor-noise
training views, recomputing quality without a supplied fault identity. The principal



                                            156
Chapter 6. REASSEMBLE Case Study: Representation, Fusion, and Robustness


adaptive-minus-static contrast over six seen-family conditions is AP +0.0097
[−.0034, .0228], unresolved. Strong blur gives adaptive AP 0.3968 versus static 0.4256;
eleven dropped channels give 0.2128 versus 0.2924. Sensor Gaussian noise at one
training standard deviation favors augmented adaptation, 0.2081 versus augmented
static 0.1749, but clean-trained F2 is 0.2246 under this later realization protocol. These
selective wins do not establish general robustness, and none of the clean noninferiority
claims passes the specified 0.01 margin.
       A later observable-quality pilot uses the selected frozen16 recipe on 753 actions,
with 77 failures in 26 recordings. Its historical static clean AP is 0.307929; the changed
branch, training and calibration recipe distinguishes this value from the restricted
original F2 and the hundred-epoch screen. It matches augmentation exposure across
three heads: static A, additive-quality B and probability–quality interaction C. All use
3,777 OOF parent actions and 11,331 augmented rows, weighted 0.50 clean, 0.25 visual
fault and 0.25 sensor fault per parent. Eight quality proxies enter the relevant models;
A, B and C have 3, 11 and 27 parameters. C has unrestricted interaction and direct
quality coeﬀicients, rather than convex reliability weights.
       The primary mean AP across declared Gaussian conditions is 0.253996 for A,
0.237360 for B and 0.257009 for C; clean AP is 0.307397, 0.292965 and 0.312168. C
minus A is +0.003012 [−0.026711, +0.032165] for the primary mean and +0.004771
[−0.034258, +0.044544] clean. Neither robustness superiority nor clean preservation at
the pilot’s zero-loss margin is established. B minus A deteriorates by −0.016636
[−0.030676, −0.002429] in a secondary contrast on the primary metric. The primary
average covers repeats, severities and two Gaussian families; it is not AP computed after
pooling repeated versions as independent subjects. Complete absence routes all heads
identically to the survivor, while both absent yields unavailable predictions. Quality can
encode action content as well as error risk, and the different capacities plus absent
quality-only/content-matched controls leave the reliability mechanism unresolved.




                                           157
Chapter 6. REASSEMBLE Case Study: Representation, Fusion, and Robustness


6.9.6     Resource-Constrained Screens and Their Interpretation

The hundred-epoch pilot uses mutually disjoint roles: 1,752 training, 419 validation, 686
calibration, 920 inner-bank and 753 assessment actions, summing to the common 4,530
population. Their recording counts are 60, 15, 19, 28 and 26. Saved prediction action
identifiers take precedence over a stale metadata field listing inner-bank recordings as
assessment recordings. This correction changes interpretation of provenance, not the
saved scores.
        The full hundred-epoch arm records about 23.96 training hours and 25.82 active
process hours. The twenty-epoch cap records about 126.23 training hours across
twenty-five fits; 137.47 hours includes validation/inference. Different accounting units
and populations prevent treating these totals as intrinsic model costs. Removed derived
caches also make a cold reproduction differ from historical cached timing. The
completed subset screen supplies a bounded paired comparison, while five-fold or
multi-seed performance requires the corresponding predictions.



6.10       Study Synthesis and Remaining Questions

The follow-ups distinguish representation suitability, complementary information and
fusion effectiveness. Longer PatchTST training improves AP by .0297 (95%
recording-bootstrap CI [.0122, .0451]) but remains .1441 below statistics. Insuﬀicient
training therefore contributes to the initial gap without fully explaining it under this
recipe. Yet their same-sensor combination improves on statistics by .0110 [.0054, .0181].
A weaker standalone representation can retain useful complementary predictions; this is
not fusion of two sensing modalities.
        Last-quarter visual pooling raises AP by .0885 [.0424, .1293] with the same fitted
head size as whole-action pooling. Additional head parameters cannot explain that
contrast. Concentrated outcome cues or reduced dilution are plausible explanations, but
neither was isolated, and the observation still extends to completion. The selected
pooling variant also improves its separate F2 comparison by .0461 [.0260, .0632]. The


                                           158
Chapter 6. REASSEMBLE Case Study: Representation, Fusion, and Robustness


spatial-encoder and matched adaptation studies show that additional processing or
updated weights need not improve the downstream combination; inconclusive
adaptation intervals do not establish equivalence.
       Core F2 includes verified within-fold visual ordering gains, while its advantage
over averaging remains unresolved. Anchored adjustment improves clean AP over its
anchor and a constant-adjustment control, but lower recall and higher calibration error
qualify the gain. Matched corruption exposure leaves the principal adaptive robustness
effects unresolved. Changing coeﬀicients therefore does not establish recognition of
unreliable inputs. Known absence is separate: the common routing rule fixes the
surviving prediction.
       These interventions were assessed separately on an already examined population.
No run combines boundary pooling, the same-sensor hybrid and anchored adaptation.
Their strongest individual results do not define an evaluated final architecture. New
recordings, natural faults, earlier prediction horizons and uncertainty beyond the fixed
fits remain open.




                                           159
Chapter 7

MSM Case Study: Dataset
Adaptation and Evaluation
Feasibility


7.1     Motivation and Intended Comparison

In MSM milling, the logistic sensor model ranked abnormal intervals more accurately
than the stacker (F2) that combined its predictions with video. Both models predicted
the same labelled intervals under the same grouping rules. Moving from assembly to
milling changed the measurements, prediction interval and target.
       The study connects the original questions in a controlled sequence. The frozen
CNN and interval statistics test a lightweight representation choice (SQ1); averaging,
original F2 and supplementary quality gates test fusion alternatives (SQ2); controlled
input stresses and explicit absence fallbacks address a bounded part of robustness
(SQ3). Target semantics, grouped assessment, probability quality and the distinction
between software feasibility and predictive value address SQ4. None of these
comparisons establishes early warning before an abnormal cut or transfer without
target-domain fitting.
       The pretrained-initialization comparison covered three fixed training seeds and



                                          160
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


the specified target, grouping and corruption conditions. An initial development run
tested the implementation on a small sample. Performance estimates came from the
subsequent held-out intervals that met the timing and input-coverage criteria.
       A close manufacturing comparator illustrates why the unimodal reference is
essential. The tool-wear study in X. Zhang et al. (2026) combines post-pass tool
photographs with acoustic information and reports its proposed MAE below a visual
baseline, while other multimodal architectures perform substantially worse than that
same visual baseline. Its wear-regression task, observation schedule and incompletely
reported tool-split coverage differ from MSM. It motivates testing the contribution of a
second stream; it does not supply a transferable numerical expectation of improvement.



7.2     Dataset Overview and Target Semantics

The source dataset combines laboratory and industrial metal-cutting observations (Kim
et al., 2026). It is a data descriptor, rather than a published unimodal-versus-fusion
benchmark. Its streams have different sampling and refresh rates, and the smartphone
camera is available only in the laboratory material. The evaluated release is the pinned
Kaggle version 2 (Manufuturetoday, 2025). The retained laboratory material covers
VM20i and VMX30Ui; the industrial VF10 component is outside this visual comparison
because it does not supply the corresponding video. The source overview describes 56
laboratory experiments. Membership in that inventory is distinct from admission to the
final paired cohort.
       Native labels distinguish stable normal cutting, intervention-worthy abnormal
cutting, and stable cutting with a defective tool. Their values are respectively 0, 1 and
2. The primary binary outcome is label 1 against labels 0 and 2. A negative outcome
therefore means stable cutting under this definition; it does not certify a healthy tool.
Expert annotation uses process behavior, sound and measurements, so the label is an
observed process judgment rather than an independently instrumented future-failure
endpoint. Relabeling every defective-tool interval as abnormal would change the task.



                                           161
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


The study instead repeats the pipeline after excluding label 2 and retains that as a
distinct target sensitivity.
       Of 2,512 native labeled intervals, 1,895 satisfy the joint video and
numerical-input requirements, including 336 positives. The remaining 617 retain their
original labels and explicit exclusion reasons. One prediction refers to one completed
cutting interval. Neither the whole interval nor its final label is shortened into a
prediction horizon that would establish advance warning.



7.3     Dataset Challenges

Milling video can show the machine and process context while coolant, enclosure
geometry and occlusion conceal the tool–workpiece interaction. Successful decoding is
therefore insuﬀicient evidence that the labeled abnormality is optically observable. A
visual classifier may respond to operating context, lighting or experiment-specific
appearance rather than the underlying cutting event. The substantial visual
training–assessment gap reported below is compatible with this concern, but does not
identify its cause.
       The machine stream is also an observation of the process rather than direct,
instantaneous ground truth. Aggregated electrical, load and vibration-related
measurements can describe an interval while smoothing short events. Their timing and
update cadence limit any claim about event onset or control latency. Group identity
creates a further diﬀiculty: experiment folders provide a useful split boundary, but they
are not verified identities for physical tools, workpieces or sessions. These limits
motivate both the technical gates and the stricter grouping sensitivities.



7.4     Preparation and Pre-Checks

Preparation admits examples only when both modalities meet a fixed input contract.
The dominant exclusion is lack of validated acoustic timing support, followed by
rejected or insuﬀicient alignment and numerical gaps. These are technical exclusions,

                                            162
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


not removals based on model correctness. The exclusion record identifies intervals
removed before modelling. Successful decoding alone does not establish that an
anomaly is visible.


7.4.1     Video Timing and Interval Correspondence

The final adapter reuses the corrected Stage 2/5 acoustic calibration rather than fitting
timing offsets against outcome labels. Its convention is video time equal to sensor time
plus the accepted offset, within the region supported by the calibration. A documented
correction matters here: VMX9 uses an offset of +49.73 s; the rejected +16.04 s alias is
not an interchangeable alternative. Treating the two alignments as equivalent would
change which visual evidence accompanies each native interval.
        Cuts use half-open intervals and uncertainty guards. Each admitted interval has
sixteen ordered, distinct native frames with source timestamps and content identities.
The assessment concerns the accepted support, not every point of the original recording.
An accepted offset supplies a defensible correspondence under the recorded calibration;
it does not prove hardware synchronization or eliminate residual timing uncertainty.


7.4.2     Visual Coverage and Geometry

The frozen recipe corrects the source sample aspect ratio of 3:8 and uses
aspect-preserving, centered black letterboxing to 640 × 640. This preserves geometry
instead of stretching narrow process views into a square. Sixteen interval frames pass
through the same CNN, and spatial and temporal means produce one interval
representation. Frame order is retained in provenance but is not learned by the
mean-pooling model.
        At least sixteen distinct native frames are required; repeating a short clip to
reach the nominal frame count is not accepted as additional temporal coverage. Later
stress conditions alter actual image content before feature extraction. Neither the
common crop nor a displayed context view is a detector box, a tool annotation, or a
verified abnormal region. The derived galleries support examination of the evidence and

                                            163
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


errors; they do not replace independent frame-level annotations.


7.4.3     Sensor Coverage and Measurement Interpretation

The evaluated numerical input comprises fifteen MTConnect channels: power, spindle
speed and load, feed rate, axis loads, and the supplied RMS-related channels. Six
channel-major statistics—mean, population standard deviation, RMS, minimum,
maximum and peak-to-peak—yield ninety values per interval. The channel names and
measurement semantics remain those of the release; they are not recast as a new
calibrated force signal.
        Numerical support must contain at least five finite rows, with no gap greater
than 0.5 s including interval edges. The pipeline neither interpolates an unsupported
gap nor substitutes an accelerometer branch. Means, standard deviations, imputation
values and corruption scales that require population estimation are fitted only within
the appropriate training partition. This prevents the assessment distribution from
influencing the numerical normalization, while leaving the source stream’s aggregation
and latency limitations intact.


7.4.4     Class Support and Independent Groups

The primary cohort has 45 nominal experiment groups, 17 containing positives, seven
machine/date proxies and five calendar dates. Every actual fitting, out-of-fold and
assessment partition must contain both classes. Table 7.1 records the positive/total
assessment support for all six target/grouping protocols. The folds within a protocol
partition its admitted cohort; the six protocols are sensitivities on related observations,
not six independent datasets.
        Experiment and date identifiers serve as proxies for physical independence.
Repeated tool or workpiece use across dates remains unresolved. The additional
DOWN-only, label-2-excluded comparison fails the fixed class-support requirement. The
split seeds and fold counts were fixed, leaving that comparison without an estimate.
Direction-specific held-out summaries are descriptive and cannot isolate a causal effect

Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility

of cutting direction.



7.5      Architecture Reuse and Dataset-Specific

        Adaptations

The pretrained condition (P) used the ResNet18-vd CNN backbone inside the
RT-DETR-R18vd checkpoint (Y. Zhao et al., 2024). The extractor stopped at the CNN
feature maps, before the hybrid Transformer encoder, detection decoder, and box heads.
Spatial means from the last three maps yielded 896 features per frame. Averaging
across sixteen frames gave one vector per cutting interval. With the backbone frozen, a
newly fitted 896 → 128 → 1 head with ReLU and dropout 0.1 supplied the target
prediction. It had 114,945 trainable parameters.
       The numerical branch is a fresh balanced L2 logistic regression on the ninety
statistics, with C = 1. A separately fitted histogram gradient boosting model provides
the stronger sensor control. It is essential to keep that control separate: F2 consumes
the logistic branch, not the HGB predictions. Averaging combines the two branch
probabilities without a fitted fusion parameter. Original F2 is a newly fitted unweighted
L2 logistic model on visual and sensor probabilities generated out of fold. No source
failure head, scaler, calibrator or fusion coeﬀicient is transferred.
       The clean and augmented quality gates are additional comparisons. They reuse a
small softmax gate over branch logits, with label-free image-quality summaries,



                                              165
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility




Figure 7.1
Paired Camera Frames and Sensor Measurements for an MSM Milling Interval

imi_vm20i/dataset27/row25, pair P03, at 112.405–113.941 s. The recorded label is 0 (stable
normal). Within-interval z scores scale these curves for display. The fitted model used
training-only normalization and assigned one prediction to the whole interval.




                                           166
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


numerical variability, missing-channel fractions and availability flags. These summaries
may encode process context as well as degradation. A changing allocation therefore is
not a calibrated estimate of modality reliability. This matters because a
reliability-aware fusion argument requires a quality proxy that actually predicts error,
rather than a proxy that merely changes with the input (Q. Zhang et al., 2023).
QG-clean uses clean OOF views. QG-aug sees clean views plus seven predefined
lowest-severity corruptions, while F2-aug is the exposure-matched static control. Clean
fitting, corruption exposure and quality conditioning remain separate interventions.
       R denotes a different question: initialization from a specifically completed
REASSEMBLE-learned CNN. The exact canonical source required by MSM’s gate was
unavailable in the inspected evaluation, so that condition remains unmeasured. This
does not contradict the P completion or the later, differently specified ViFailback donor
study. Architecture reuse, generic pretraining and transfer of task-learned weights
retain separate provenance.



7.6     Implementation Checks and Bounded

        Development Runs

The bounded development exercise was an engineering check: its two-epoch,
at-most-24-interval design restricted head updates and CNN frame forwards and
prohibited outer-assessment predictions. It could expose shape, gradient, memory or
reload errors, but could not estimate generalization. Generalization was assessed in the
subsequent evaluation on held-out intervals.
       The saved implementation audit checks planned cases, source identities, native
assessment units, model budgets and checkpoint reloads. It also refits the fixed F2
estimator from saved clean OOF probabilities and reports zero coeﬀicient discrepancy.
The CNN cache check reports maximum difference zero on an outer-training-only
example. These support consistency of the saved procedure and its outputs. Their
scope is narrower than reproduction of stochastic training: exact reload and metric


                                           167
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


recomputation cannot alone certify that every training step would recur in another
environment.



7.7     Evaluation Protocol and Conditions Without

        Estimates

The completed matrix uses three outer and two inner StratifiedGroupKFold partitions,
fixed split seeds and training seeds 42, 123 and 20261023. Inner fits produce branch
OOF probabilities for learned fusion; outer assessments remain excluded from fitting.
The six target/grouping protocols yield 54 completed case records. The 54 cases include
different folds and seeds from overlapping protocols.
       The main visual head runs for ten fixed epochs and the gate for forty. The
assessment uses a fixed threshold of 0.5, with no assessment-selected epoch or fitted
primary calibrator. Scikit-learn computes average precision as a weighted mean of
precision values, with each weight equal to the increase in recall (Pedregosa et al., 2011).
This differs from trapezoidal integration of the precision–recall curve. F2 denotes the
fusion model in this study. Primary results pool the disjoint outer predictions separately
for each seed, then summarize the three APs. An arithmetic mean of fold APs answers
a different question because fold prevalences and cross-fold score scales differ.
       Conditions with a missing source checkpoint, inadequate class support or
insuﬀicient temporal coverage yielded no estimate. The results below cover the
conditions that met the original criteria.



7.8     Findings, Limitations, and Implications

On intact inputs, F2 had lower AP than its logistic sensor component at all three seeds.
The mean paired difference was −0.071737 (Table 7.2). F2 ranked abnormal intervals
more accurately than the visual branch or equal averaging. Sensor HGB had the highest
mean AP of the models compared.

       Logistic sensing had higher mean AP than F2 in all six prespecified protocols.
Excluding label 2 and grouping by calendar date gave 0.521826 for F2 and 0.891656 for
logistic sensing. That sensitivity analysis changes the target. Under the primary
definition, pooled prevalence was 336/1895 = 0.177309 and outer-fold prevalences
ranged from approximately 0.051 to 0.277. The fold-specific training-prior baseline had
pooled AP 0.145395 because the fitted prior could vary across folds.
       The paired 95% calendar-date bootstrap interval for F2 minus logistic sensing
was wholly negative, [−0.100356, −0.051477]. It used five date clusters, held fits and
split assignments fixed and could not account for unidentified equipment reuse.
QG-clean averaged +0.008802 AP above logistic sensing, with one negative seed and an
interval of [−0.097085, +0.056426]. The interval admits gains and losses and leaves
equivalence unresolved.
       At the highest sensor-noise level, QG-aug reached 0.298636 mean AP, close to
intact vision at 0.298901. F2 reached 0.206863 and noisy sensor HGB reached 0.606157.
Under the strongest image blur, QG-aug achieved 0.682784, below F2 at 0.771420 and
intact logistic sensing at 0.828616. The comparison therefore depends on which stream
deteriorates and which predictor remains available. The tests also covered brightness
and contrast changes, occlusion, frame omission, zeroed channels and explicitly missing
channels.
       Whole-modality absence invokes a separately programmed surviving-branch
fallback: 0.828616 AP without vision and 0.298901 without sensors. Equal fallback

                                            169
Chapter 7. MSM Case Study: Dataset Adaptation and Evaluation Feasibility


scores across fusion methods are imposed behavior, not adaptive recovery learned from
data. Differences in augmentation exposure between a gate and its static control would
confound the effect of quality conditioning.
       For seed 42, F2 had lower AP than logistic sensing but a lower Brier score
(0.056091 versus 0.061724) and higher F1 (0.820438 versus 0.811268). QG-clean had
higher AP than F2, a worse Brier score of 0.064406 and a lower F1 of 0.786421. Thus
the ranking comparison favored logistic sensing, whereas probability error and
fixed-threshold decisions favored F2 for this seed. The relative performance of the gates
depended on the input condition.
       The visual model ranked held-out intervals poorly despite a much stronger
training result. Inadequate features, limited event visibility, residual alignment error
and experiment-specific appearance could each contribute. Independent annotations of
event visibility and a matched representation comparison on the same groups would
help distinguish these explanations.

Chapter 8

ViFailback Case Study: Application
to a Further Dataset


8.1     Motivation and Replication Objectives

On held-out ViFailback manipulation tasks, the logistic sensor model ranked episode
outcomes more accurately than the fitted combination of video and sensor scores. The
dataset provides three camera views and robot observations for complete episodes (Zeng
et al., 2026). The study compared the two branch predictions and their combination on
the same tasks (SQ1 and SQ2). A separate comparison isolated the choice between a
generic frozen visual encoder and one initialized from a REASSEMBLE checkpoint,
with a new prediction head fitted in each case.
       The primary replication contrasts original frozen vision, logistic sensing,
averaging and F2. HGB supplied a stronger numerical reference, while camera ablations
and donor transfer tested visual representations. The primary comparison was specified
on 30 October 2026 after some early results had been observed. Its interpretation is
therefore partly retrospective.
       The reporting cutoff for this revision is extended through the completed-scope
analysis of 24 November 2026. In addition to the frozen and donor comparisons,
FULL20 completed seed 42, outer fold 0 and its three inner fits; FULL100 completed its



                                           171
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


single-role pilot. The researcher then stopped further training. The remaining
target-CNN matrix and SQ3 corruption study are unfinished, not negative model
results. All reported comparisons concern intact inputs.



8.2     Dataset Overview and Prediction Task

The native source contains 5,202 episodes with 4,545 failure and 657 no-failure
annotations across 100 canonical tasks and 109 source directories. The evaluation pins
both the original release and its converted LeRobot representation (sii-rhos-ai, 2025).
Matching version identities matters because the paper, source directory layout,
converted observations and admitted model cohort describe different levels of the
evidence.
       The source paper evaluates visual-symbol and visual-language reasoning,
including open-ended failure explanations and policy interventions. Its model does not
consume the 280 engineered robot-observation features used here. Its benchmark also
differs in task, cohort and metric: a reported visual-language benchmark score is not
failure-positive or both-class AP for this classifier. The paper therefore provides dataset
and completed-outcome context, rather than a directly comparable fusion score.
       The final cohort contains 4,382 episodes, including 3,753 failures and 629
successes across 99 canonical tasks. Failure is the positive class, giving prevalence
0.856458. An episode contributes one target, although it supplies 48 sampled RGB
images. The task is retrospective prediction of native whole-episode annotations. It is
not frame-level failure localization, verification of a task’s final mechanical success
independently of annotation, or advance warning before a failure occurs.



8.3     Dataset Challenges and Cohort Construction

The provisional core contained 4,384 episodes after 818 unique exclusions or
quarantines. Full three-camera quality checks then identified two defective camera
streams: episode 2071’s right wrist and episode 4784’s left wrist. Their source bytes

                                            172
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


remain preserved. Removing them from analysis produces the final 4,382
common-support episodes and 820 unique exclusions. Reason counts can overlap;
summing them as if disjoint would overstate the excluded population.
       The records distinguish exact trajectory/video duplication and invalid telemetry
from low visual quality and uncertain semantics. Duplication and invalid observations
can invalidate the input contract; visual diﬀiculty does not justify excluding examples
after inspecting correctness. Coordinate task 55 remains quarantined rather than
receiving a guessed sign correction. Agreement of converted 25 Hz observations does not
independently certify original hardware synchronization. Canonical task separation also
leaves operator/session identity and semantic similarity between tasks unresolved,
limiting the independence claim to the grouping actually checked.



8.4     Preparation and Pre-Checks

Sixteen uniform, matching frame indices are selected in each of three cameras. These
are sixteen temporal positions and 48 images, not 48 distinct timestamps. The source
image processor resizes to 640 × 640, divides RGB values by 255 and does not apply
ImageNet normalization. Equal camera and temporal means remove order information
from the episode embedding. A fixed crop shown in an example is context visualization,
not a detected object or validated failure location.
       The sensor adapter retains fourteen position, twelve nonplaceholder velocity and
two gripper-effort fields. Ten statistics per channel produce 280 values. These
observations are not calibrated wrench or force/torque measurements. Labels, failure
categories, action commands, keyframes and task/source identifiers do not enter
ordinary model features. Per-episode feature calculation is separated from population
fitting: standardizers use T only, and calibration uses disjoint C examples.
       The twenty-example visual pilot was selected for observability and balance.
Nineteen decided observations agreed with native labels; B02 remained uncertain. This
is a bounded project examination, not an independent human-rated prevalence sample



                                           173
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


or a held-out classifier benchmark. The additional automated consistency checks are
also not a signed second-human review. Technical decoding and a successful
native-label join do not resolve every episode’s semantic meaning.



8.5     Reused Components and Dataset-Specific

        Adaptations

The original visual branch loaded the ResNet18-vd CNN backbone inside the pretrained
RT-DETR-R18vd checkpoint (Y. Zhao et al., 2024). The extractor stopped at the CNN
feature maps, before the hybrid Transformer encoder, detection decoder, and box heads.
Spatial means of three maps with 128, 256, and 512 channels yielded 896 features per
image. The branch averaged these features across frames and cameras and fitted a new
896 → 128 → 1 head to predict the episode outcome. Three-view pooling, the sensor
statistics, and the task partitions were specific to ViFailback.
       The logistic sensor branch uses balanced L2 regression with C = 1, training-only
scaling and a separately fitted sigmoid calibrator. Averaging and F2 consume its
calibrated probabilities alongside the visual branch. F2 is unweighted L2 logistic
probability stacking; HGB is a stronger separate reference and never replaces the sensor
input inside original F2. Consequently, a gain over vision or logistic sensing cannot be
reported as a gain over the strongest available unimodal method.
       The donor arms transferred compatible CNN tensors from a completed source
checkpoint and fitted a fresh ViFailback head, scaler, calibrator and fusion model. The
FROZEN and DONOR conditions held the CNN weights fixed. The FULL conditions
train the executed CNN convolutions and normalization aﬀine terms while holding
running moments fixed; their completed coverage is stated below. This is CNN
adaptation, not training the detector encoder, decoder or detection heads. The
completed DONOR comparison therefore measures supervised target-head fitting on a
transferred frozen representation.




                                            174
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


8.6     Evaluation Protocol

The original protocol uses three outer task-disjoint folds and three inner OOF
assessments within each outer-training pool. Each branch separates gradient-fitting T,
checkpoint-selection V, calibration C and assessment A task groups. A final branch is
not automatically fitted to the entire outer-training pool: its T/V/C roles remain
distinct. The inner assessment probabilities build a meta bank without using examples
that fitted, selected or calibrated their generating branch.
       Checkpoint selection uses validation macro AP; the selected checkpoint is then
calibrated on C, and assessment uses a fixed threshold of 0.5. Failure AP, success AP
and their arithmetic mean are retained. With failure prevalence above 85%,
failure-positive AP alone can conceal poor success discrimination. Pooled AP is
calculated from disjoint outer predictions, separately for each seed. It is not the
arithmetic mean of fold APs, and seed repetition does not add independent episodes.
       The later matched twenty-epoch arms use the first twenty epochs of a fixed
cosine-50 schedule, while the hundred-epoch arms use cosine-100. Highest V macro AP
selects the checkpoint, then lower V BCE and the earliest epoch resolve ties. All
configured epochs are intended to execute even when the chosen checkpoint is earlier.
The twenty-epoch study has a complete three-inner OOF bank. The hundred-epoch
pilot instead reserves 788 meta episodes under one predeclared role, followed by 1,107
outer assessments. It is not a full nested three-fold hundred-epoch experiment.



8.7     Experimental Investigations

The original study evaluated the frozen visual branch, its high-camera control, sensor
models and clean fusion. By 24 November, the matched study had 79 completed fits of
111 planned: 36 each for FROZEN20 and DONOR20, four for FULL20, and one for
each hundred-epoch pilot. These support 22 outer fusion assessments: eighteen
frozen/donor twenty-epoch assessments, one FULL20 assessment and three pilots.
Conditions and seeds reuse episodes. The 32 remaining FULL20 fits contribute no


                                           175
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


performance estimates.

8.7.2     Original Fusion-Procedure Comparison

In the original frozen comparison, F2 improves over vision and averaging but has lower
pooled macro AP than the logistic sensor branch for all three seeds: 0.628787, 0.625400
and 0.621067 against 0.648974. The sensor HGB point score is higher still. These
results support a narrower conclusion than “fusion works”: the numerical information
can improve weak visual prediction, but adding the visual branch does not improve the
strongest component used in F2.
        The later matched twenty-epoch comparison also favored the numerical reference.

negative comparison, not proof that every visual representation will damage fusion.

8.7.3     Representation-Transfer Comparison

Following donor feature extraction, DONOR20 completed 36 target-head fits. Its source
completed 100 epochs and selected epoch 47; 115 compatible CNN tensors are loaded,
without source head, scaler, calibrator or F2 coeﬀicients. This establishes an executed
learned-representation transfer experiment with fresh target supervision. The fixed
donor is one source-training realization; three target seeds do not replicate donor
selection or training.
        It does not establish a consistent predictive advantage. Relative to FROZEN20,

                                             177
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


donor visual pooled macro-AP differences are −0.016497, −0.006219 and +0.004573.
Their saved task-cluster intervals cross zero. Donor F2 differences are mixed:
−0.001530, +0.010813 and +0.006381. F2 with the transferred encoder achieved higher
macro-AP than F2 with the frozen encoder for two of the three target seeds. The
sensor-only logistic model achieved higher macro-AP than the transferred-encoder F2
model for all three seeds.
       On the same 1,107 outer episodes, the hundred-epoch frozen-backbone pilot
achieved macro AP of 0.550995 for vision and 0.694219 for F2. This arm reused the
completed historical head-training trajectory after verifying the same checkpoint
selection. The donor-backbone pilot achieved 0.571448 and 0.696600, respectively. Both
pilots used a single seed and fit role, with a separate 788-episode bank for fitting fusion.
Neither pilot updated the target CNN.

8.7.4     Scope of the Robustness Evaluation

ViFailback contributes clean-input comparisons to SQ1 and SQ2. The SQ3 corruption
study remained unexecuted at the extended reporting cutoff. The specified visual
interventions included missing frames or windows, blur, occlusion, camera loss, JPEG
degradation and within-episode timing shifts. Sensor interventions included missing
channels or intervals, Gaussian noise, frozen readings and timing shifts. The matched
CNN comparison contained no quality gate. Performance under these interventions
remains a question for future work.
        The specified availability rules route complete single-modality absence to the
surviving branch and abstain when both inputs are absent. These rules do not estimate
reliability from data. The edge-padded sampling shifts simulate timing errors without
measuring drift between hardware clocks.


8.7.5     Additional Exploratory Baselines

HGB uses 200 iterations, learning rate 0.05, L2 penalty 0.1 and minimum leaf size
twenty; leaves 7, 15 and 31 are selected on V macro AP, followed by C-only calibration.
Its search is real candidate selection, unlike the fixed MSM recipe or a claim that every
parameter was optimized. It remains secondary and does not change the prespecified F2
input after its favorable assessment.
        The high-camera control fitted a new head on sixteen high-view frames under
the original cohort and split rules. The original frozen study also evaluated a clean
quality-conditioned gate and its availability-only control. Estimating the effect of
corruption exposure would require the deferred augmented and stress comparisons.



8.8      Results, Interpretation, and Limitations

In the original comparison, F2 ranked failures (AP near 0.95) more accurately than
successes (Table 8.2). In outer fold 0 of the matched FROZEN20 comparison, seed 42
gave F2 macro AP of 0.707118 and a Brier score of 0.153928, yet balanced accuracy was


                                           179
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


0.5 at the fixed threshold. Selecting a threshold on those same assessment episodes
would bias the estimate of decision performance.
       Some fitted sigmoid calibrators reversed the ranking of episodes within a fit
because their slopes were negative. Even positive slopes can change the ordering of
episodes from different folds when the fitted maps differ. Comparing raw and calibrated
rankings helps interpret differences between pooled AP and mean-fold AP.
       The three donor-minus-frozen visual contrasts had task-cluster intervals
spanning zero. These paired intervals used 2,000 draws and held the fitted models,
donor and split assignments fixed. They omit uncertainty from refitting, donor selection
and possible dependence within sessions or operators. The exploratory comparisons had
no multiplicity adjustment. Coeﬀicients were fixed before outer scoring, although the
labels and some tasks had already been examined during development.
       The complete twenty-epoch frozen/donor comparison addresses representation
transfer. The November 24 extension additionally assesses target adaptation on one
outer fold, with inconclusive paired effects. The rest of the adaptation matrix remains
unfinished, and no completed matched corruption comparison establishes robustness.



8.9     Relationship to the Earlier Case Studies

In MSM, the logistic sensor model achieved higher abnormal-cutting AP, averaged over
seed-specific pooled predictions, than the original stacker. In ViFailback, it achieved
higher macro-AP in both the original comparison and the later matched frozen
comparison. In HANDOVER, the logistic sensor model also outperformed averaging.
Missing participant identities precluded a learned-fusion comparison. The direction of
the effect can be compared across these tasks, although their absolute AP values refer
to different outcomes.
       Transferring the donor CNN produced mixed effects across target seeds. For
complete ViFailback episodes under the task-disjoint protocol, the logistic sensor
predictor ranked outcomes more accurately than F2 in the matched twenty-epoch



                                           180
Chapter 8. ViFailback Case Study: Application to a Further Dataset


comparison. The completed adaptation contrasts do not establish a change in that
conclusion, but cannot resolve adaptation across the full cohort. Effects of input
degradation remain unmeasured.




                                           181
Chapter 9

Integrated Discussion

The welding proposal asked how a process-aware multimodal architecture could be
designed and evaluated for industrial anomaly detection. It called for a comparison of
what the camera, acoustic signal, and machine measurements could predict about weld
quality. The project moved to PHM gear monitoring and REASSEMBLE robotic
assembly. When the Intel Welding data were subsequently reconsidered, the feasibility
audit documented prior use of the proposed test population (Chapter 1 and
Appendix G). That population could no longer supply an independent estimate of
weld-quality performance.
       Combining predictions improved failure ranking over the sensor component in
REASSEMBLE assembly. The comparison used a common action outcome and held out
entire recordings. In MSM milling and ViFailback manipulation, stacking ranked
outcomes less accurately than its sensor component. In HANDOVER, equal averaging
likewise fell below sensor-only prediction. In PHM, the interpretation of prediction error
depended on how closely the photograph-derived measure corresponded to gear damage.
Each comparison used the same target and held-out observations within its study.
Extending the comparison to welding requires welds reserved for assessment and an
independently measured quality outcome.




                                           182
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


9.1     Representation Suitability — Answer to SQ1

In PHM, the image-regression experiment extracted detector features during a full
prediction pass. In the principal sensor experiment, post-run photographs instead
supplied the continuous target. A patch-based Transformer encoded sequences
summarizing speed, torque, temperature, vibration and condition indicators. For SQ1,
the regression error measures how well this representation predicts the photographic
proxy. Assessing physical tooth damage would require a separate reference (Chapter 5).
       Across 4,530 completed REASSEMBLE actions, sensor statistics achieved pooled
failure AP .429, against .242 for the initial scratch-trained PatchTST and .231 for frozen
RT-DETR-R18vd CNN features with mean pooling (Table 6.1). In the matched budget
follow-up, longer PatchTST training raised AP from .2557 to .2853, an increment of
.0297 (95% CI [.0122, .0451]); it remained below statistics. Combining the two sensor
representations reached .4403, an increment of .0110 [.0054, .0181] over statistics. Thus,
the temporal representation was weaker alone yet useful in combination. This result
concerns representations of the same sensors, not two sensing modalities.
       Last-quarter pooling increased visual AP by .0885 [.0424, .1293] with unchanged
head size; its simultaneous lower bound over the five pooling contrasts was .0301.
Greater visibility of outcome cues near completion and reduced dilution are competing
explanations, rather than isolated mechanisms. The rule still averages frames and does
not demonstrate learned temporal order or early warning. Matched two-epoch
adaptation gave opposite visual effects across two seeds, with intervals spanning zero;
the hundred-epoch one-fold F2 increment was .0141 [−.0008, .0291] (Appendix D).
These results distinguish budget, aggregation and adaptation, without testing their
best-performing choices jointly.
       In MSM, the frozen camera representation had mean AP of .299 for abnormal
milling intervals. Ninety sensor statistics yielded .829 with logistic regression and .901
with histogram gradient boosting (HGB). Weak visual performance could reflect limited
visibility of the event or how the frozen encoder represented it. The study did not
isolate these explanations (Table 7.2). ViFailback pooled three camera views per

                                            183
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


episode and summarized 28 robot-observation channels. Transferring a visual encoder
trained on REASSEMBLE produced mixed changes across target-training seeds relative
to generic frozen features (Table 8.3). A newly fitted head could use the transferred
features, but their predictive advantage varied across seeds.
       HANDOVER provided a further test of representation on a shared observation
window. Sixteen video frames and 36 force–torque statistics described the same portion
of each trial. The wrench was measured on one robot and estimated from joint torques
on the other. Mean failure AP was .772 for vision and .824 for logistic sensing on the
oﬀicial test trials (Table H.1). Across the five studies, compact sensor summaries
provided strong references, and frozen convolutional features supplied a reusable visual
input whose value depended on the task and time window. Published acoustic
representations remain candidates for the original welding objective. None of the five
studies establishes which audio encoder to deploy, and the scratch-trained time-series
comparisons leave the benefit of pretrained time-series weights open.



9.2     Comparative Fusion Performance — Answer to

        SQ2

In PHM, concatenating the embeddings of three sensor groups gave a mean absolute
error of 0.824 percentage points on the photographic proxy, compared with 0.804 for the
training mean when entire experiments were held out. The paired interval allowed
either model to have lower error (Table 5.2). This comparison addresses sensor-feature
fusion under SQ2. A separate four-arm study included an aﬀine combination and a
context gate. Its normalizer was fitted before the evaluation split loop, which
compromises the isolation of held-out observations within that diagnostic. The principal
leave-one-experiment-out comparison used a separate training-only normalizer.
       REASSEMBLE supplied the most direct comparison of the four approaches.
Across 148 held-out recordings evaluated in five folds, concatenation achieved .412
failure AP, the statistical sensor predictor .429, equal probability averaging .446, and


                                           184
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


logistic stacking (F2) .447. The original gates ranged from .441 to .445 (Tables 6.1
and 6.2). The paired AP difference between stacking and sensors was .0179 (95%
recording-level bootstrap CI [.0086, .0283]). Against averaging, the difference was .0016
(95% CI [−.0076, .0112]). The fitted stacker ranked failures more accurately than its
sensor component. The interval for stacking versus averaging included gains and losses
in failure ranking.
       The saved-score audit identifies a narrower visual contribution directly: all
sensor coeﬀicients are positive, and removing the visual term preserves sensor AP and
AUROC within each fold. Restoring it improves AUROC in five of five folds and AP in
four, with AP differences from −.00469 to +.03945. Those changes cannot arise from a
strictly increasing transformation of the same sensor scores. Fold-specific
transformations nevertheless alter pooled ranking: reduced-score AP is .43092 versus
sensor AP .42939. The missing refitted sensor-only second-stage control therefore limits
attribution of the complete pooled increment, without negating the within-fold evidence.
This is conditional usefulness in the evaluated models, not causal modality importance
or a population-wide information claim.
       On MSM, mean abnormal-cutting AP over three seeds was .757 for stacking,
.736 for averaging, .829 for its logistic sensor component and .901 for the separate HGB
reference. The paired stacking-minus-logistic contrast was −.0717 (95% CI [−.1004,
−.0515]), conditional on the saved fits and five date clusters. Figure 9.1 shows the
reversal of the stacking–sensor comparison between assembly and milling. ViFailback
gave a similar direction for a different endpoint. Original stacking attained pooled
macro-AP of .621–.629 across three seeds versus .649 for logistic sensing (Table 8.2 and
Chapter 8). Macro-AP here weights success and failure equally. In HANDOVER,
averaging the two branch predictions gave mean failure AP of .803 on 151 oﬀicial test
trials, below the sensor component at .824. The missing participant-to-trial mapping
prevented construction of the participant-disjoint predictions required to fit the planned
stacker. HANDOVER therefore supplies a measured comparison for averaging and an
open question for learned fusion. Across these tasks, the clearest gain occurred for


                                           185
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


simple prediction fusion in assembly. The two other stacking studies and the handover
averaging study each favored their constituent sensor model on the primary ranking
metric.
Figure 9.1
Prediction Performance With Intact Inputs by Study




Note. REASSEMBLE uses pooled failure average precision for 4,530 actions. MSM uses mean
abnormal-cutting AP over three seeds, each score calculated from pooled outer-fold
predictions. The panels have different targets, prevalence, and evaluation protocols. The panel
values and their sources are listed in Appendix F, E107.




9.3        Robustness and Reliability — Answer to SQ3

SQ3 asks whether a gate improves robustness when inputs are missing, degraded, or
unreliable. A known outage and a plausible but erroneous reading require different
responses. PHM illustrates the distinction even within the sensor stream. In its
separate four-arm diagnostic, one nominal group had no input columns, so dropping it
removed no information. The gate was computed from context, so corrupting inputs to
the other branches could leave its weights unchanged. Small changes around an already
weak regression predictor give limited evidence about reliability. The photographs
supplied the target in the principal comparison, leaving inference-time camera loss
outside that experiment.
          In REASSEMBLE, known complete loss of a stream routed the prediction to the
surviving branch. Every fusion rule using that policy inherited the survivor’s score.

                                             186
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


When both streams remained present and the sensor stream received Gaussian noise at
one training standard deviation, stacking gave AP of .222 and the quality-only and
combined gates gave .175 and .179, respectively. Intact vision alone achieved .231.
Responding to noise by changing a weight therefore did not ensure a better failure
ranking. The result pertains to this corruption and severity.
       The anchored adjustment raises clean REASSEMBLE AP from .4473 to .4683:
an increment of .0210 (95% CI [.0015, .0419]) over its static anchor and .0226 [.0036,
.0432] over a constant-adjustment control. The latter narrows the explanation beyond
another fixed coeﬀicient change, although capacity differs and context need not measure
reliability. Under matched corruption exposure, the mean adaptive increment over six
conditions is .0097 [−.0034, .0228]. The separate exposure-matched quality-interaction
pilot gives .0030 [−.0267, .0322]. Both primary robustness effects and their
clean-performance requirements remain unresolved. Matching exposure removes one
source of unfairness, not differences in capacity or optimization. These studies establish
a particular clean gain without demonstrating consistent recognition of degraded but
present inputs.
       In MSM, the strongest sensor noise reduced stacking to .207 AP. The
augmentation-trained gate reached .299, approximately the score of intact vision alone.
Under the strongest image blur, the same gate reached .683, below stacking at .771 and
intact logistic sensing at .829. In HANDOVER, the strongest blur left averaging at .779
versus .824 for intact logistic sensing. Known complete loss used an explicit fallback.
The participant grouping requirement prevented evaluation of a learned gate there
(Appendix H). ViFailback evaluated a clean camera-view ablation. The SQ3 corruption
tests were outside its final computational budget, leaving the effect of degradation on its
predictors unmeasured.
       REASSEMBLE, MSM, and HANDOVER implemented a fallback to the
surviving predictor when a stream was declared absent. Across the five studies, a
consistent predictive gain from adaptive weighting under degraded but present inputs
remains unresolved. A fair test gives the static and adaptive models the same


                                           187
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


corruption exposure and calibration opportunities, then compares each with the intact
or degraded single stream available in that condition. Whether such a gate improves
weld-quality decisions under sensing failures remains open.



9.4     Evaluation Beyond Accuracy — Answer to SQ4

The PHM error measures agreement with image-derived candidate area in percentage
points per run. For SQ4, interpreting that agreement as physical damage requires an
independent measurement. Twenty nominal runs came from three gear experiments.
Holding out a whole experiment tests whether the predictor generalizes beyond runs
from experiments represented during fitting. Uncertainty estimated from saved run
predictions remains conditional on the fitted models.
       REASSEMBLE shows how to distinguish failure ranking, probability calibration,
and the threshold at which an operator would act. The positive AP increment over
sensors did not resolve every recall or calibration contrast. A review of 64 selected
action clips found 57 agreements among 59 determinate judgments, with five
indeterminate and two disagreements. The review exposed uncertainty about outcome
visibility and boundaries. Its stratified sample and single reviewer limit population-level
estimates of annotation accuracy (Appendix E). Recorded timings for the visual, sensor,
and fusion components describe a warmed computation with prepared inputs.
Operational latency also includes acquisition, completion of the observation window,
and the response to a prediction.
       At seed 42 in MSM, stacking had a lower Brier score than logistic sensing (.056
versus .062), even though logistic sensing ranked abnormal intervals better. In
ViFailback, failure prevalence exceeded 85%. At seed 42, stacking achieved failure AP of
.945 and success AP of .312, yielding macro-AP of .629 (Table 8.2). Reporting both
classes exposed a weakness that the majority-class score concealed. Task-cluster
intervals characterize variation over the assessed tasks conditional on the fitted models.
Session and operator dependence remain additional sources of uncertainty.



                                           188
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


       In HANDOVER, 110 of the 151 test trials were failures. At a threshold of .5, a
training-prior predictor labelled every trial a failure, achieving failure F1 of .843 and
zero specificity for successes. The oﬀicial split remained usable for evaluating fixed
baselines, while unavailable participant identifiers prevented participant-level
uncertainty estimates for their contrasts. These examples support reporting prediction
time, label provenance, class-specific discrimination, probability error, threshold
consequences, and the grouping used for uncertainty. The branch predictions and fitted
coeﬀicients show how each stream contributed to a fused score. Establishing whether
operators interpret it correctly or can act in time would require prospective observation
and a usability study.



9.5     Agreement and Disagreement With Previous

        Research

Pretrained spatial features are reasonable candidates because PatchCore demonstrates
useful industrial inspection from intermediate CNN features, while RT-DETR learns a
multiscale hierarchy for detection (Roth et al., 2022; Y. Zhao et al., 2024). Their
positive findings do not imply that every downstream component helps. The matched
REASSEMBLE spatial-encoder comparison favored CNN-based F2 over the hybrid
representation by .0158 AP (95% CI [.0058, .0248]). Different feature widths and head
sizes prevent attribution solely to attention. This limits transfer of the detector’s
architectural conclusions to action outcomes; it does not contradict its detection
benchmark.
       PatchTST’s favorable forecasting ablation supports local patch tokens and
channel-shared temporal encoding (Nie et al., 2023). Our longer-budget result confirms
that optimization duration affected the scratch classifier, yet statistics remained
stronger. Their successful same-sensor combination supports complementary predictive
use rather than replacement by the higher-scoring representation. Classical geometry’s
competitiveness in inspection is consistent with taking compact, task-relevant


                                            189
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


summaries seriously (Horwitz & Hoshen, 2023); it does not establish why these
particular robot statistics work. Likewise, TimeSformer’s divided spatial–temporal
attention motivates examining temporal organization (Bertasius et al., 2021). The
boundary-pooling gain supports sensitivity to aggregation, but uses an order-invariant
mean within a selected interval and does not test that learned-attention mechanism.
       The positive REASSEMBLE F2 contrast and negative external contrasts agree
with a conditional view of fusion. Welding’s image-only, audio-only and fused
penetration classifiers all reached 100% clean accuracy (Hua et al., 2026, Tables 1–3).
Published HANDOVER five-class accuracy fell from 69.1% with video and force/torque
to 67.9% after adding gripper state (Thoduka et al., 2024b, Table III); our binary,
differently fitted AP comparison is not a replication. Other settings do show gains:
acoustic–thermal gearbox monitoring reports 99.54% against 90.54% for thermal alone
under a random within-condition split (Ying et al., 2025, Table 2). Such results disagree
with an unconditional prediction of a gain here, not with the bounded negative findings
in MSM or ViFailback. Wang and colleagues identify unequal overfitting and
optimization diﬀiculties in jointly trained multimodal networks (W. Wang et al., 2020).
They supply plausible competing mechanisms, not a demonstrated cause of losses in our
separately fitted branches and stacker.
       Stacking’s use of cross-validated probability outputs motivates a small learned
combiner (Ting & Witten, 1999). Our within-fold audit establishes beneficial visual
ordering changes in the saved F2, while its pooled advantage over averaging remains
unresolved. This distinguishes complementary predictions from evidence that learning
the combiner is necessary. It also explains why the coeﬀicients cannot be read as causal
modality percentages.
       Adaptive weighting has a positive precedent: QMF links useful uncertainty
weighting to a generalization bound and reports gains under specified RGB–depth
corruption (Q. Zhang et al., 2023). Our anchored clean increment, .0210 [.0015, .0419],
supports a particular context-dependent adjustment, whereas the matched-exposure
robustness increment, .0097 [−.0034, .0228], remains unresolved. This is neither a


                                          190
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


replication nor a refutation of QMF: the quality variables, links, training procedures
and targets differ. It identifies what does not transfer automatically from its result.
Lower clean recall and higher ECE after anchored adjustment further show that
improved AP is not calibrated reliability. This agrees with evidence that clean
calibration need not survive shift (Ovadia et al., 2019). Missing-input and
simulated-robot studies address other access regimes (Altinses & Schwung, 2023; Deng
et al., 2026); shared fallback cannot establish adaptive fault recognition.



9.6     Lessons Across Datasets and Limits of

        Comparison

Table 9.1 returns to the main research question. The units range from gear runs to
individual actions, milling intervals, manipulation episodes, and handovers. Within each
study, the single-input and fused models can be compared directly. Differences in
targets, observation windows, class frequencies, and splits preclude a common ranking
across datasets.
       The target definitions help interpret these differences without establishing their
causes. REASSEMBLE labels completed actions: 509 of 4,530 are failures (11.24%). Its
sixteen sampled frames and progress-resampled sensor channels cover the action, and
recording-disjoint assessment supports the observed within-fold visual contribution.
Boundary pooling demonstrates sensitivity to where visual evidence is retained. It does
not show that a camera would help at an earlier decision time.
       MSM instead distinguishes native intervention-requiring abnormal cutting (336
intervals) from stable-normal (1,434) and stable-defective (125) cutting: prevalence is
17.73%. Sixteen frames and ninety statistics of fifteen controller channels describe the
admitted cutting window. The target can remain negative despite a defective tool; it is
not the robotic failure endpoint. Strong sensor performance, including HGB, supports
informativeness of this processed telemetry for that mapping. Weak frozen vision may
reflect visibility, alignment error or representation mismatch; none is isolated.

Estimated alignment is not hardware synchronization. Sensitivity analyses vary label
treatment and grouping, while five acquisition dates constrain the paired uncertainty.
         ViFailback labels whole episodes, with 3,753 failures among 4,382 cases (85.65%).
Three-camera means and 280 summaries of robot positions, velocities and gripper effort
discard most temporal and view-specific structure; these numerical observations are not
wrench measurements. Task-disjoint assessment and both-class macro AP expose weak
success ranking that high failure AP conceals. Completed donor transfer and limited
target adaptation do not establish a visual advantage. Limited representation
suitability, redundant predictions or different observability of native outcomes remain
competing hypotheses.
         HANDOVER uses shared trial windows, sixteen frames and 36 wrench
summaries; one robot measures wrench while the other estimates it from joint torques.
Its oﬀicial test contains 110 failures among 151 trials (72.85%). Averaging
underperforms logistic sensing on that cohort, but missing participant mapping blocks



                                              192
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


the mandated OOF fitting of learned fusion. This is an eligibility limit, not an observed
failure of a gate. Differences in prevalence also alter AP baselines across studies, so
absolute scores cannot identify which task or architecture is intrinsically easier.
       All external predictors receive local outcome supervision. MSM and
HANDOVER reuse the architecture and refit predictors; ViFailback additionally
transfers REASSEMBLE-supervised CNN weights and fits fresh target components.
None tests an unchanged source model on an unseen installation. Figure 9.2 locates
these bounded comparisons within the literature synthesis.



9.7     Threats to Validity and Unresolved Evidence

Three features of the data limit a causal interpretation of architectural differences.
First, the target may only approximate the phenomenon of interest. PHM has no
independent quantitative tooth-damage reference, MSM’s label mapping defines which
intervals count as abnormal, and some robotic outcomes remained ambiguous under
visual review. Second, the number of independently acquired units can be much smaller
than the number of predictions. Gear experiments, recording sessions, dates, tasks, and
participants capture different sources of dependence. Where identifiers were missing,
independence across those groups could not be verified. Third, several later analyses
revisited assessment populations examined during development. Intervals conditional on
fitted predictions and variation over a few seeds do not capture that selection history.
       Preprocessing before the separate four-arm PHM loop limits attribution of its
results to architecture. In other experiments, differences in calibration, capacity,
training budget or corruption exposure accompanied architectural changes. Some
historical configurations lack the detail needed to reconstruct training exactly.
ViFailback’s complete frozen/donor matrix is supplemented by the November 24
one-fold FULL20 comparison and FULL100 pilot. Their adaptation effects remain
inconclusive; the remaining adaptation fits and corruption study are unexecuted
(Chapter 8). The literature synthesis covers the full texts available to a single reviewer.



                                            193
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion




Figure 9.2
Literature Comparisons and Experimental Coverage




Note. The middle column summarizes prior research. The right column gives the
corresponding experimental findings and limits.




                                           194
Chapter 9. Integrated Discussion


       Finally, the completed-window studies allowed the predictor to observe evidence
near the outcome. Synthetic blur, noise, or stream removal test specified interventions.
They give limited information about gradual calibration drift, unobserved timing faults,
or shifts to another physical machine. Transferring a predictor to an earlier decision
time or a different installation requires observations at that time and place.



9.8     Implications for Multimodal System Design

In a monitoring application, the intended decision determines what constitutes failure,
how it can be confirmed independently, and which observations arrive in time to be
useful. Evaluating each stream on the same held-out units establishes what it can
predict alone. Equal averaging and a fitted static combination then provide references
for testing whether context-dependent weights add value. When an instrument declares
an outage, the remaining predictor supplies a natural reference. When faulty values still
appear plausible, the relevant test is whether the gate recognizes situations in which
trusting that stream would worsen the prediction.
       A prospective return to welding could reserve whole welding sessions, collect an
independent weld-quality measure, and fix the prediction horizon before examining
those outcomes. On the same welds, exposure- and calibration-matched models could
be assessed for clean performance, known absence, degraded readings, timing error, and
shifts between operating conditions. Paired effects and a prespecified tolerance for
clean-performance loss would clarify the operational cost of any robustness gain. Such a
test would show whether the modest gain for completed assembly actions carries over to
weld-quality decisions.




                                           195
Chapter 10

Conclusion and Future Work


10.1      Answer to the Main Research Question

The study began with two questions about weld quality. Could a camera reveal changes
that machine signals missed? Would the combined prediction remain reliable if either
input deteriorated? The architecture comparison extended to gear monitoring and
robotic assembly. By the time the Intel Welding data were re-examined, development
had already drawn on the proposed test recordings. Independent welding validation
would require a new set of welds. The five studies evaluated related questions against
the outcomes available in each dataset (Appendix G).
       In PHM, post-run photographs supplied a continuous measure of candidate
damage area on gear teeth. The photographs defined the target for sensor regression.
Across held-out experiments, combining three sensor groups gave a mean absolute error
of 0.824 percentage points, compared with 0.804 for predicting the training mean
(Table 5.2). The paired interval allowed either model to have lower error. Even an
accurate prediction of this proxy would still require independent measurements to
establish how closely the photographed area tracked physical damage.
       REASSEMBLE made a direct fusion comparison possible. Video and machine
signals covered the same completed action, each action had a recorded outcome, and
entire recordings could be held out for assessment. Across 4,530 actions from 148



                                          196
Chapter 10. Conclusion and Future Work


recordings, logistic stacking achieved failure average precision (AP) of .447, compared
with .429 for its sensor component and .446 for equal probability averaging (Tables 6.1
and 6.2). The paired increment over sensors was .0179 (95% recording-bootstrap CI
[.0086, .0283]); the increment over averaging, .0016 [−.0076, .0112], remained
unresolved. Independent saved-score analysis verifies that vision improves within-fold
AUROC in all five folds and AP in four, beyond strictly increasing recalibration of the
same sensor score. Cross-fold score alignment and the absent refitted sensor-only
second-stage control still limit attribution of the full pooled gain. These intervals
condition on the saved fits, not the complete selection process.
       The external studies tested whether the same finding held for different targets.
In MSM, stacking ranked abnormal milling intervals less accurately than the logistic
sensor model, with mean abnormal-cutting AP of .757 and .829, respectively; the paired
difference was −.0717 [−.1004, −.0515] conditional on five date clusters (Table 7.2). In
ViFailback, the target was the outcome of a whole manipulation episode. Across three
seeds, stacking yielded macro-AP of .621–.629 against .649 for logistic sensing.
Initializing the visual encoder from REASSEMBLE gave mixed results across the
matched runs, while the completed November 24 one-fold target-adaptation contrasts
remained inconclusive (Tables 8.2, 8.3 and 8.4). In HANDOVER, averaging video and
force–torque predictions on 151 trials yielded mean failure AP of .803, below the sensor
component at .824 (Table H.1). Participant-to-trial identifiers were unavailable, so the
supplementary study could not construct the participant-disjoint predictions needed to
train and evaluate its proposed stacker.
       Taken together, the studies motivate modular evaluation: measure each stream’s
contribution before combining predictions. This is a design recommendation supported
by contrasting outcomes, not experimentally demonstrated superiority of a single
modular architecture. The value of that combination depended on the target, the
available observations, and the strength of the sensor predictor. The results concern
gear-condition estimation and retrospective classification of completed operations.
Independent weld-quality detection remains the unanswered application of the original


                                            197
Chapter 10. Conclusion and Future Work


question.



10.2        Supported Contributions

SQ1 is partially answered for the evaluated tasks and budgets. REASSEMBLE
statistics outperformed the scratch-trained PatchTST even after its matched training
extension, yet their combination improved AP by .0110 [.0054, .0181]. The frozen
ResNet18-vd CNN supplied a reusable spatial representation; last-quarter pooling
improved visual AP by .0885 [.0424, .1293] with unchanged head size. These findings
establish conditional suitability and complementary predictive use, not universal
preference for statistics, freezing or a Transformer. Learned temporal order, pretrained
time-series weights, audio and earlier prediction remain unresolved.
       SQ2 is answered comparatively within the assessed recipes, but remains open as
a general architecture-selection rule. The shared assembly cohort allowed feature
concatenation, probability averaging, logistic stacking, and scalar gates conditioned on
process context to be evaluated on the same actions. Feature concatenation reached AP
of .412. The simpler prediction-level combinations ranked failures more accurately under
the tested training procedures. The external results qualify that finding by identifying
tasks on which even the learned combination fell below its sensor component.
       For missing or degraded inputs (SQ3), a known missing stream could be handled
by routing predictions to the available model. When a stream remained present but
became unreliable, the two original gates evaluated under corruption showed no
consistent advantage. Under strong sensor noise in REASSEMBLE, stacking fell to AP
.222, below the intact visual predictor at .231. Anchored adaptation improved clean AP
by .0210 [.0015, .0419], but the matched-exposure robustness increment was .0097
[−.0034, .0228]. SQ3 is therefore only partially answered: explicit fallback is established,
while consistent adaptive benefit under degraded present inputs remains unresolved.
This gate and the later visual pooling method were evaluated in separate experiments.
       SQ4 is answered at the retrospective evaluation level: distinguishing ranking,



                                           198
Chapter 10. Conclusion and Future Work


probability error, class-specific decisions and uncertainty changes the interpretation of
performance. Prospective utility and end-to-end deployment performance remain
untested. On the seed-42 MSM comparison, stacking yielded a lower Brier score than
logistic sensing (.056 and .062, respectively), although sensing ranked abnormal
intervals more accurately. Grouped analyses, uncertainty estimates, a review of action
labels, and component timings exposed different limits on reliability and practical use.
The code and configuration records identify which branches and fusion rules produced
these results. The literature matrix places them alongside prior welding, camera–sensor,
and missing-input studies and narrows the open question to the performance of
adaptive weighting under comparable training and calibration conditions.



10.3      Principal Limitations

Each dataset imposes a different evidential limit. PHM lacks an independent physical
reference, and the preprocessing order in its separate four-arm diagnostic prevents a
leakage-free interpretation of that experiment. The industrial and robotic datasets offer
incomplete information about independence at the level of tools, objects, or
participants. Complete-action and complete-episode predictions can use evidence that
only becomes visible near the outcome. Deploying a warning system would require a
prediction horizon that excludes such information.
       The comparison also depends on how the models were fitted. Calibration,
training budgets, and exposure to corruptions differed in some comparisons. Several
follow-ups revisited previously evaluated populations, and the reported bootstrap
intervals condition on the fitted models. The short-budget fine-tuning comparison did
not resolve a mechanism for the observed differences. The reporting cutoff includes the
November 24 ViFailback closure: 79 of 111 matched fits completed, with 32 FULL20 fits
unfinished and the corruption evaluation unexecuted. Completed adaptation intervals
include gains and losses; unfinished work supplies no negative model result. Where only
run summaries were available, they limited the scope for independent checks of



                                           199
Chapter 10. Conclusion and Future Work


individual predictions.



10.4       Priorities for Future Research

An independent welding study is the most direct next test of the original question. It
should specify the weld-level outcome and prediction time, pair the camera and process
measurements to that decision, and reserve complete new welding sessions for
assessment. An independent inspection should establish weld quality. For PHM, a
physical measure of damage linked to a known tooth would likewise distinguish
measurement validity from the ability to predict the photographic proxy. Independent
review of ambiguous robotic outcomes would strengthen the corresponding classification
targets.
       Within each study, the informative comparison is between the strongest
single-input predictor, equal averaging, a fitted static combination, and a gate trained
with comparable calibration opportunities and corruption exposure. Repeated tests of
known absence, unannounced corruption, temporal misalignment, and shifts between
physical groups would show which failure conditions justify adaptive weighting.
Prespecified margins for clean performance and grouped uncertainty estimates would
make the cost of a robustness improvement visible.
       The remaining transfer experiments require matched optimization budgets,
normalization procedures, and held-out tasks. For operational monitoring, prospective
tests should also measure warning time, false alarms, and end-to-end delay, and
evaluate how operators act on uncertain predictions. These measurements would
establish whether the modest ranking benefit observed on completed assembly actions
translates into a useful decision while a weld is still in progress.
