The author defined the scope of the research, provided the implementation records and
saved experimental results, obtained full-text publications, and reviewed how the
methods and findings were interpreted and presented. Preparation of the manuscript
involved AI-assisted drafting and revision. The author used AI tools to develop and
refine the text, including the abstract, and to examine whether descriptions of the
models, experiments, and results were consistent with the available evidence.
       The author conducted the literature research and critically reviewed the papers,
assessing how their methods, choice of baselines, and evaluation conditions supported
their findings. AI tools helped the author prioritise papers for closer reading, locate
relevant passages and tables during review, and organise the evidence into a comparison
matrix. During the November revision, the author obtained additional publications and
used AI assistance to match their titles and DOIs to Zotero records and check the
corresponding PDF attachments. AI tools also supported numerical consistency checks,
bibliography processing, APA citation editing, figure preparation, LaTeX editing and
compilation, and the extraction of chapter text.
       The manuscript revision drew on existing publications, implementation records,
and saved experimental outputs, without new model training or inference on raw data.
The thesis distinguishes findings reported in publications, checks documented in earlier
experiment records, and checks performed on saved outputs during revision. The 25
November revision used Codex to cross-map existing literature and controlled
experiments, recompute the core F2 ranking diagnostic, reconcile the November 24
ViFailback closure and revise a preserved manuscript copy. Baseline hashes, source
identities, the revision diff and actual validation outcomes accompany that copy.
AI-assisted appraisal formed part of the same review process and provided no
independent second human assessment. Ambiguous source claims, incomplete
experiments, and unresolved questions of physical validation are documented as
limitations.

                                         217
Appendix A

Literature Search Records and
Evidence Tables


A.1         Discovery Population and Evidence Status

The screening record dated 16 November 2026 lists 120 unique candidates. Of these, 39
met the inclusion criteria, 37 were excluded and 44 remained potentially relevant but
insuﬀiciently verified. The corresponding full-text appraisal covered 37 of the 39
included works. The two accessible only through abstracts and bibliographic metadata
contributed to the screening counts but supplied no methodological or numerical
findings.
       The subsequent appraisal covered 59 PDF files. Two pairs were byte-identical,
leaving 57 distinct files. Titles, authors, DOIs and repository identifiers showed that
these represented 57 additional works, bringing the total appraised to 94. This
historical total includes empirical industrial studies, transferable methods, statistical
and representation methods, dataset descriptions and contextual studies. The 25
November revision adds one separately identified methodological reference, Ting and
Witten (1999), to address the missing original foundation for stacked generalization. It
is stored in references_additions.bib and is outside the two-stage appraisal counts
and matrices; the review was not restarted.



                                            218
Appendix A. Literature Search Records and Evidence Tables


       The search log for this screening round records more than 15 distinct queries.
Several queries returned results together, precluding an exact count of the records
retrieved by each. The subsequent appraisal drew on additional accessible full texts and
used publisher or repository pages to check bibliographic identities and versions. This
procedure limits the review’s coverage to the works identified and accessible through
these routes.



A.2      Extraction, Locators, and Versions

For each study, the appraisal recorded the prediction unit and target, physical inputs,
derived representations, fusion method, single-input comparisons and assignment of
data to training and evaluation. Missing inputs, noisy inputs and changes of operating
conditions were coded separately. Numerical findings cite the relevant page, table or
figure. Where the available text left a procedure unspecified, its status was recorded as
unknown.
       The appraisal distinguishes the published bibliographic record from the version
examined. Preprints and journal articles sometimes give different sample counts for the
same work. File hashes identify the version used for each extraction. The 37 initial
full-text records include 148 short quotations that locate the evidence for their codes.
The review compared reported methods and results without rerunning the published
experiments.



A.3      Comparison Matrix and Denominators

Table A.1 gives the codes for the initial 37 studies. Table A.2 identifies the contribution
and principal limitation of each of the 57 additional works. The accompanying matrix
gives the missing-input, noise and shift tests for individual studies, together with source
locations.
       Within-protocol input variants do not guarantee equal capacity, pretraining,
calibration or selection budget. Planned single-input deployment, missing training

                                           219
Appendix A. Literature Search Records and Evidence Tables


images, generated substitution, stochastic dropout and unexpected inference outage are
different conditions. Ordinary defective objects, natural geometric gaps and fixed noisy
data are not automatically controlled corruption experiments. The codes describe this
inspected evidence and cannot estimate field-wide prevalence.

Interpretation: Transferable methods are non-industrial unless their record identifies a
direct application. Dataset papers and unimodal/context references do not supply a
multimodal fusion experiment. A retained preprint remains provisional. All source
versions, complete qualifications and numerical locators are in the expanded register.



A.4       Benchmark Identity and Assessment

          Populations

The MVTec 3D-AD dataset paper lists 249 anomaly-free and 948 anomalous test
samples, a total of 1,197. It reports 2,656 training and 294 validation samples
separately (Bergmann et al., 2022, Table 1, PDF p. 4). UCF reports the same test
count (Cheng et al., 2025), whereas some other method descriptions give 1,137. The
discrepancy leaves the samples underlying those comparisons uncertain. The review
uses the published scores, whose comparability also depends on preprocessing, masks
and metric definitions.

B.2        Target Lineage and Pairing
The PHM target phm2026_image_damage_v3 uses one deterministically selected view for

each of teeth 5–28 and aggregates the three highest tooth values for each run. This gives a

provisional image-derived proxy for physical deterioration. Earlier targets used different image

geometry, view aggregation and tooth sets, which limits comparison of their errors. Sorting

tooth values removes positional identity, so the profile describes their distribution within a

run. The fixed image split and the leave-one-experiment-out sensor evaluation assess different

populations.

       The REASSEMBLE release reports 4,551 demonstrations. The analysis included 4,530

completed pick, insert, remove and place actions after applying the primary-action and

input-completeness criteria. Of these, 509 were failures. Each action supplied 22 scalar sensor

channels and a descriptor from 16 hand-camera frames. The core experiment excluded audio

because the correspondence between annotation times and waveform times could not be

independently established.




                                               228
Appendix B. Dataset Inventories, Exclusions, and Target Definitions


B.3       External Cohorts and Exclusions
MSM’s primary target is intervention-worthy abnormal behaviour, native label 1, against

labels 0 and 2. Label 2 describes stable cutting with a defective tool, rather than simply

belonging to the same semantic class as label 1. A sensitivity excluding 125 label 2 intervals

gives 1770 observations. The 45 nominal experiment groups, seven machine/date proxies and

five calendar dates are not interchangeable grouping identities; the date-level evidence has

very few independent clusters. Technical exclusions establish a usable cohort, not a claim that

the excluded intervals were uninformative.

        ViFailback contains 5,202 native episodes, with 4,545 failures and 657 successes. Two

camera-quality exclusions reduced a provisional cohort of 4,384 episodes to 4,382. The 820

excluded or quarantined episodes count each episode once even when several exclusion reasons

apply. The historical and later matched CNN conditions assess the same 4,382 episodes under

different fitting procedures. Repeated seeds and outer folds reuse these episodes.

        The HANDOVER study included 587 of 589 native trials. In two training trials the

nearest wrench observation exceeded the 0.1 s alignment limit. The 151 test trials contain 110

failures and 41 successes. The higher failure prevalence than in REASSEMBLE affects

comparisons of failure-class average precision. Eligibility depended on the native partition and

availability of both modalities. Participant-level independence could not be verified for

individual trials.

Appendix C

Model Configurations and
Implementation Details


C.1      Executed Component Boundaries
The PHM scalar-image regressor and the REASSEMBLE action classifier use different

components of RT-DETR. Table C.1 identifies the model weights, intermediate representation

and output used by each branch.

Table C.1
Inputs and Outputs of the Executed Model Components

  Component          Input and representation            Executed boundary
  PHM scalar         Three spatially pooled              Ultralytics RT-DETR-L prediction
  image branch       256-dimensional maps, plus          path with hooked maps; not the
                     recorded extraction latency:        CNN-only REASSEMBLE path
                     769 actual head covariates
  REASSEMBLE         Sixteen RGB frames pooled to        Frozen ResNet18-vd CNN
  visual branch      an 896-dimensional action           backbone inside RT-DETR-R18vd,
                     descriptor                          followed by a trained binary head
  REASSEMBLE         22 channels, ten statistics each:   Training-only standardization and
  numerical          220 features                        logistic regression
  statistics
  REASSEMBLE         512 progress positions by 22     Scratch-initialized compact
  temporal branch    channels                         PatchTST and fitted
                                                      projection/head

  numerical          ViFailback: 280 statistics       construction; not an unchanged
  adapters                                            raw-sequence temporal encoder
Note. The reported dimensions apply to the implementation paths inspected.



                                            230
Appendix C. Model Configurations and Implementation Details


       The PHM scalar-image head receives extraction latency alongside pooled image

features. The dependence of latency on the computing environment limits attribution of the

regressor’s performance to image content. The target measures candidate area in photographs.

Earlier target versions selected the maximum across views, while the retained version uses a

fixed view for each tooth. The top-three run summary aggregates the tooth values.

Independent physical defect masks were unavailable to validate localization.



C.2      Temporal Representations
The PHM implementation defines a compact channel-independent PatchTST-style regressor

with default patch length sixteen, stride eight, hidden width 32, four attention heads, two

encoder layers, feed-forward width 64, dropout 0.1 and a 64-unit head. The configured input

width remains run-specific. A scalar output and the optional sorted-profile head answer

different target questions. The latter uses a base and positive softplus increments to enforce

ascending order; this guarantees an order-statistic profile but discards tooth identity. The

monotonicity does not demonstrate that the estimated profile matches actual damage.

       The REASSEMBLE temporal branch follows a separate configuration: patch length

32, stride sixteen, width 32, two layers, four heads, feed-forward width 64 and dropout 0.1. It

begins without pretrained temporal weights. Mean patch pooling and channel concatenation

feed a 128-dimensional projection and binary head, yielding 109,505 parameters in the

inspected implementation. Observed-value masks are retained and passed through the relevant

interface, but that does not establish attention suppression of every missing token. Missing

values are filled under training-only normalization. The source-specific mask semantics

therefore remain part of the implementation limitation rather than being inferred from the

model family (Nie et al., 2023).



C.3      Heads, Fitting and Calibration
The original visual head is a new 896–128–1 network with ReLU and dropout 0.1, containing

114,945 trainable parameters above a frozen backbone. Original visual epoch candidates are

five and ten; temporal candidates are three and six. Both use AdamW with learning rate

0.001, weight decay 0.001 and batch size 128. These candidates constitute the recorded

                                              231
Appendix C. Model Configurations and Implementation Details


selection procedure, not a broad optimizer search. Logistic regression separately compares

C ∈ {0.1, 1, 10} under inner-fold selection.

       Branch calibration used an unweighted sigmoid logistic fit to the selected inner

out-of-fold scores. The outer assessment labels were withheld from calibration. The original

procedure selected a threshold between 0.01 and 0.99 by maximizing inner out-of-fold balanced

accuracy, resolving ties toward 0.5. Later studies used a fixed threshold of 0.5. Comparisons

between those procedures therefore combine the effects of model and threshold changes.



C.4        Fusion Mechanisms and Attribution
Uniform fusion averages the two calibrated branch probabilities. F2 fits an unweighted L2

logistic model to those probabilities, with C = 1 and a new intercept and two coeﬀicients. It is

neither an F-beta metric nor a convex weighted average. Its coeﬀicients depend on score

scaling and are not causal percentages of modality importance.

       Feature fusion F3 combines a 128-dimensional visual projection with the 220 statistical

numerical features through separate projections, normalization and a fitted classifier. Its

recorded candidates use hidden widths 32 or 64 and ten or twenty epochs. Context gates

instead allocate weights over branch logits. Their candidates have hidden width eight or

sixteen and forty epochs. Action-only, quality-only and combined contexts remain different

models, as do a static gate with context held constant and a gate exposed to corrupted OOF

records.

       With one branch available, the explicitly masked convex-logit gate gives that branch a

weight of one. The resulting prediction follows from the specified masking rule. When both

branches are absent, some implementations reject the input and the external evaluation

records abstention and coverage.

       The later anchored adjustment is another distinct mechanism. It modifies frozen F2

probability coeﬀicients by bounded context-dependent factors, with a zero-initialized adaptive

output. It is not the same convex logit gate and need not have the same calibration or

threshold policy. Appropriate static and exposure-matched controls are necessary to identify

what, if anything, adaptation adds.




                                               232
Appendix C. Model Configurations and Implementation Details


C.5       External Adaptations
MSM and HANDOVER use the shared frozen visual backbone with a newly trained head and

a dataset-specific numerical classifier. ViFailback adds pooling across three camera views. Its

matched study compares frozen generic and transferred CNN weights with a fresh target head

in each case. Scalers, sigmoid calibrators and out-of-fold fusion coeﬀicients were fitted to

target data where specified. The separate condition for updating all target CNN weights was

incomplete at the November assessment.




                                               233
Appendix D

Supplementary Results and
Sensitivity Analyses


D.1       Matched Two-Epoch Representation Diagnosis
The frozen32 versus fine-tuned32 diagnosis compares equal epoch and data budgets on the

complete 4,530-action REASSEMBLE cohort. It contains five matched outer folds for seed 42

and five for seed 20261023, with 75 new and 25 explicitly reused visual fits. Equal budgets do

not imply equal parameter counts, computation or training time. The fixed numerical branch

is shared between arms, so a visual or fusion difference does not arise from refitting a different

sensor model.

       Table D.1 gives pooled failure-positive AP for each method at both seeds. The

intervals resample paired recording clusters with the models held fixed. Mean-fold AP is a

separate summary that weights folds differently.

Table D.1
Fine-Tuned Minus Frozen Average Precision After Two Epochs

     Seed        Method         Frozen    Fine-tuned    Difference [95% interval]
     42          Visual       0.222708      0.230609    +0.007902 [−0.017312, +0.033219]
     42          Averaging    0.444720      0.440946    −0.003774 [−0.014238, +0.007851]
     42          F2           0.439363      0.438566    −0.000796 [−0.006424, +0.006072]
     20261023    Visual       0.216799      0.200517    −0.016282 [−0.039636, +0.009790]
     20261023    Averaging    0.436495      0.428489    −0.008006 [−0.018180, +0.003279]
     20261023    F2           0.435202      0.432380    −0.002822 [−0.008285, +0.003602]
Note. The conditional 95% intervals quantify contrasts between the fitted predictors.
Variation across training seeds is a separate source of uncertainty.


                                               234
Appendix D. Supplementary Results and Sensitivity Analyses


       At seed 42, the small gain in visual AP coincided with lower AP for both fusion

methods. At the second seed, fine-tuning reduced the point estimate for all three methods.

Every interval includes zero, so the direction of an adaptation effect remains uncertain.

       The diagnostic compared training duration, complementary errors, branch calibration,

F2 fitting and the difference between out-of-fold and assessment scores. The available

comparisons did not identify a cause for the variation across seeds. Class-conditional Brier

score and log loss characterize prediction errors on actions the sensor misclassified, but cannot

determine whether those images contained suﬀicient visual evidence.



D.2       The Same-Test 753-Action Follow-up
The 753-action cohort is exactly original outer fold 0, not an independent external dataset. It

contains 77 failures from 26 recordings within the full 4,530-action, 148-recording cohort. The

correspondence is established through native recording/segment identities, timestamps and

labels, with older sample hashes retained as aliases. Failure prevalence is therefore different

from that of the pooled five-fold assessment.

Table D.2
Prediction Performance on the Same 753 Held-Out Actions

                        Model/recipe                              AP
                        Original frozen visual               0.232805
                        C20 full-CNN outer refit, seed42     0.215321
                        Frozen CNN100                        0.193776
                        Full CNN100                          0.212585
                        Static sensor, original score vector 0.327705
                        Original F2 restricted to fold0      0.367155
                        Frozen CNN100 plus F2                0.334977
                        Full CNN100 plus F2                  0.349094
                        Frozen CNN100 plus averaging         0.348007
                        Full CNN100 plus averaging           0.358533
Note. Models were evaluated on identical actions with intact inputs. The training, calibration,
and stacking procedures differed between generations.


       Fine-tuning improves the hundred-epoch visual recipe relative to its frozen

hundred-epoch control, but the original frozen recipe has a higher point AP on the same

actions. Similarly, full100 F2 exceeds frozen100 F2, while the existing paired interval crosses

zero. The original F2 row uses a different nested stacking protocol. Neither comparison

establishes that the evolving later recipe is the best general architecture.

                                                235
Appendix D. Supplementary Results and Sensitivity Analyses


        The selected inner job originally fitted 1,752 training actions, selected on 419

validation actions and calibrated on 686. Its original assessment contained 920 inner actions.

The hundred-epoch follow-up retains those fitting roles but assesses the 753 original outer

actions; the 920-action bank becomes the held-out fusion-fitting bank. In contrast, the C20

outer refit uses 2,385/581/811 training/validation/calibration actions. These role differences

remain even after the assessment identities match.



D.3       Aggregation, Aliases and Sensitivity

          Boundaries
Across all five original outer folds, pooled AP was 0.230658 for vision, 0.429392 for the static

sensor and 0.447299 for F2. The C20 full-CNN model achieved pooled AP of 0.312127. The

hundred-epoch follow-up assessed only 753 of those actions and produced no predictions for

the other 3,777.

        Several apparent comparisons reuse identical predictions. The M3 sensor row contains

the original sensor vector, and the hundred-epoch fusion study uses the standalone visual

outputs. Native recording and segment keys assign the 753 outer assessment actions to 26

recordings. The 28-recording list in the selected-fit metadata refers to the inner prediction

bank.

        MSM varied the target definition and grouping policy and compared exposure to

synthetic corruption. HANDOVER evaluated its baselines without fitting learned fusion

because participant-disjoint out-of-fold predictions were unavailable. ViFailback compared the

original frozen representation and later matched donor conditions. Differences in targets,

prevalence and grouping limit any comparison of average precision across these datasets.



D.4       Saved-Prediction Check of the Core F2 Visual

          Term
The 25 November arithmetic audit checks 4,530 unique actions, their 509 failure labels, fold

assignments and separation of the 148 recording groups. Section 1B’s held-out branch columns



                                               236
Appendix D. Supplementary Results and Sensitivity Analyses


match Section 2’s reused inputs; the sensor column also matches the separately saved

statistical control. The original Section 1 visual prediction file is unavailable locally, so parity

does not extend to a replay of that file or of training. The inspected fitting source and saved

metadata order the columns as visual, then sensor.

        All visual coeﬀicients are positive (3.099–4.063), as are all sensor coeﬀicients

(6.218–6.709). Reconstructing F2 from them differs from the saved probabilities by at most

2.22 × 10−16 . Independently calculated threshold-group AP and positive–negative ordering

changes agree with the library metrics. Omitting the visual term preserves each fold’s sensor

AP and AUROC; Table D.3 gives the full-model changes.

Table D.3
Core F2 Minus Sensor Ranking Within Each Held-Out Fold

                Fold    Actions    Failures   Recordings        ∆AP     ∆AUROC
                    0       753         77             26   +.039451      +.020691
                    1       964        109             31   +.014616      +.011674
                    2     1,037        125             32   +.011329      +.024430
                    3       906         99             29   −.004689      +.002891
                    4       870         99             30   +.036423      +.020045
Note. Failure-positive metrics on saved outer predictions. Folds share training data and are
not independent experimental replications. No new training or interval estimation was
performed.


        Fold 3 has 3,051 corrected and 2,820 damaged positive–negative pairs. Its positive

AUROC change and negative AP change are consistent because AP weights retrieval positions

differently. Pooled AUROC is .779325 for sensors, .777037 after arithmetic omission and

.794968 for F2. Fold-dependent score alignment therefore affects pooled metrics without

changing within-fold sensor ordering. Setting the visual probability to zero is a particular

constant reference, not an identified decomposition of pooled importance. The reduced map

retains jointly fitted parameters; it is not a refitted sensor-only comparator.

Appendix E

Human-Review Protocol and
Supporting Materials


E.1      Review Sample and Presentation
The retained review package contains 64 clips, balanced across the four action classes and

spread across recordings. It is an audit sample, not a population-random estimate of

annotation accuracy. Fixed blind IDs and sheet order support correspondence between the

material presented and returned ratings. The browser interface supplied compatible WebM

playback copies while retaining the original MP4 files. An additional encoding can soften

visual detail, so the originals remain relevant when investigating presentation artifacts.

Package checks addressed file hashes, decoding, frame counts, cadence and interface logic; they

did not themselves establish annotation correctness.

       One reviewer returned complete ratings for all 64 clips. The submitted rating file was

checked against the blind identifiers, required fields, allowed responses and reasons for flagged

cases. The first assessment was complete at the reporting date. The flagged cases awaited

adjudication.



E.2      Single-Reviewer Results
The reviewer agreed with the recorded outcome on 57 of 59 determinate clips (96.6%) and

disagreed on two (3.4%). Five of the 64 clips had indeterminate outcomes (7.8%). Across the


                                               238
Appendix E. Human-Review Protocol and Supporting Materials


Table E.1
Reviewer Agreement With Recorded Outcomes for 64 Clips

                     Action     Agree Disagree       Indeterminate     Total
                     Pick           13           0                 3      16
                     Insert         14           1                 1      16
                     Remove         15           0                 1      16
                     Place          15           1                 0      16
                     Total          57           2                 5      64


full sample, agreement was 57 of 64 (89.1%). The 96.6% estimate is conditional on a judgeable

outcome. The 89.1% estimate includes all sampled clips. Equal sampling of each action class

limits generalization of either percentage to the full dataset.



E.3      Boundary and Presentation Flags
Boundary ratings were 60 acceptable, three questionable and one indeterminate. Visibility was

rated adequate for all 64 clips. Four clips received possible recording or presentation artifact

flags. Because flag categories overlap, their sum is not the number of affected clips: ten unique

clips require follow-up. The flag list retains reasons and exact blind IDs. An adequate

visibility rating also does not imply that the observed interval unambiguously establishes the

action outcome; five outcomes remained indeterminate despite that visibility assessment.

       Adjudication remained incomplete. The original clips and annotation boundaries

would help distinguish an ambiguous task outcome from a boundary or playback problem.

The evaluation used the original labels for all cases, including those flagged. No thresholds or

predictions were changed following the review.



E.4      Interpretation and Reviewer Independence
One reviewer supplied all ratings, so inter-rater agreement could not be estimated. The results

describe agreement between that reviewer and the recorded labels for the sampled clips.

Establishing inter-rater reliability would require a second reviewer who had not seen the first

reviewer’s ratings or interpretations.

       The author had access to generic AI-generated examples of wording. The reviewer



                                               239
Appendix E. Human-Review Protocol and Supporting Materials


supplied every clip-level rating. Verification of the submitted fields and totals contributed no

additional judgment about the clips.




                                              240
Appendix F

Experiment Provenance and
Reproducibility


F.1      Available Experiment Records
The experiment records identify the versions of datasets, model code and configurations used

in each study. They include selected predictions, training records, completion receipts and

result summaries. Some raw data, trained model weights, feature caches and historical

prediction files are held separately. The source manifests record their locations where known.

       The numerical checks used existing outputs. The original training, gradient checks and

saved-model inference belong to the recorded experiments. The checks described below did

not involve refitting a model or running new inference on raw inputs.



F.2      Numerical Verification
For REASSEMBLE, average precision was recalculated from saved predictions for the core

and specified follow-up comparisons. For MSM, the check used the primary assessment

probabilities for intact inputs and selected perturbations. For the original ViFailback

experiment it used episode-level branch and fusion probabilities. For HANDOVER, the five

clean means and seed standard deviations were recalculated from three sets of per-seed

metrics. The individual trial probabilities were unavailable in the inspected export. The PHM

comparison used the original run summary and paired-bootstrap record.


                                              241
Appendix F. Experiment Provenance and Reproducibility


F.3      Reconstructing an Evaluation Cohort
The REASSEMBLE registry links each evaluation to the native recording and segment keys of

its assessed actions. It distinguishes training, validation, calibration and assessment

membership. Some legacy identifiers are aliases. The recorded fit identity also distinguishes

runs that share an outer seed label but use different random states for model training.

       The 753 actions in the selected outer fold form a subset of the pooled 4,530-action

assessment. Native recording and segment identifiers establish common test membership

across models on the smaller set. Checkpoint selection, calibration and threshold procedures

differed between some fits on that set, limiting attribution of a score difference to architecture.

       MSM links intervals by their native IDs, label 2 policy and experiment or date group.

HANDOVER links trial IDs within the oﬀicial split and pairs video with the common wrench

window. Participant identities are unresolved. ViFailback uses the recorded original and

converted release revisions, canonical task identifiers and the final cohort with three-camera

quality checks. Its original and matched studies share 4,382 episodes but use different fitting

procedures.



F.4      Hashes and Source-State Identity
The external-source manifest gives SHA-256 digests for files at their original location and for

local copies. It also identifies the inspected Git revision. Some reports included uncommitted

changes, so the commit identifier alone cannot reconstruct those files.

       The recorded original and local hashes can differ after line-ending conversion. The

source path and corresponding hash identify which version underlies a calculation. ViFailback

completion records identify the condition, training seed and fit role for each completed

comparison in Table 8.1.



F.5      Saved-Result Checking and Experiment Replay
The archive’s integrity checks compare file digests and references in the cohort registry. Score

checks match the assessment identities, labels and probability definitions before computing

performance. Failure-positive average precision uses precision at distinct score thresholds and


                                               242
Appendix F. Experiment Provenance and Reproducibility


increments in recall. Trapezoidal area under the precision–recall curve uses a different

interpolation.

        Pooling disjoint outer-fold predictions before computing AP weights the resulting joint

ranking. Averaging AP over folds gives each fold the specified aggregation weight. Similarly,

averaging probabilities across seeds defines an ensemble, whereas averaging AP across seeds

describes variation among fitted predictors. The uncertainty estimates use the study-specific

cluster units. In HANDOVER the absence of participant identities prevents a

participant-cluster interval.

        A training replication also depends on the original raw inputs, model and configuration

versions, software dependencies and either the fitted weights or their initialization procedure,

depending on the stage being repeated. Where only a summary survives, it is possible to check

the reported aggregation but not to regenerate individual predictions from that summary.



F.6      Coverage of the Checks
The matched two-epoch REASSEMBLE comparison records new and reused fits and selected

saved-model checks on pixel inputs. Those checks leave the cause of its seed-dependent

behavior unresolved. In ViFailback, FROZEN20 and DONOR20 produced complete

three-seed assessments, while FROZEN100 and DONOR100 produced single-role pilots. The

reporting extension includes the completed one-fold FULL20 comparison and the FULL100

single-role pilot. The remaining FULL20 fits and the SQ3 corruption tests are unfinished. The

available records support verification of the completed comparisons. Independent training

replication would require a separate run.



F.7      Project Evidence File Register
Each identifier refers to one source file or dated copy. Evidence footnotes link to files with a

recorded GitHub URL. An entry marked “GitHub link TBD” has no GitHub link available.

The accompanying file index gives relative source names and the sections, code routines or

samples cited. New entries prefixed repo: in that index resolve from the research-repository

root. The preserved revision package contains baseline hashes, the working-copy diff and

current saved-score checks. The 25 November ViFailback check verifies 1,008 scalar metrics

                                               243
Appendix F. Experiment Provenance and Reproducibility


from 168 saved model/cohort vectors and the completed-fit records; its intervals remain the

recorded November 24 paired analyses, not newly repeated bootstrap estimates.

Appendix G

Research Chronology


G.1       Original Objective and Dataset Selection
The original research objective concerned process-aware multimodal architectures for robotic

welding. The exposé defined the research questions before the subsequent industrial and

robotic studies. The choice and timing of those studies were exploratory. Their relationship to

the original questions is a retrospective interpretation.



G.2       From the Welding Motivation to Two

          Task-Specific Studies
The PHM study began with image exploration and developed a continuous gear-condition

proxy. The September v3 target used one deterministic view per tooth, teeth 5–28 and a

top-three run aggregate. The earlier maximum-over-views targets and fixed image split

addressed different prediction settings. The available records do not establish the initial

reason for selecting PHM. Concerns about the validity of the proxy emerged through its

construction and evaluation.

       Re-examination of Intel Welding found intended-condition categories rather than

independently verified defects, class-dependent modality availability, recipe/category

confounding and 216 sessions crossing the supplied split. A proposed 32-session holdout had

already appeared in a whole-population context audit. That exposure invalidates an



                                               249
Appendix G. Research Chronology


untouched-test claim without proving that every model trained on those labels. The

foundation review stopped before new model training; it supplies feasibility evidence, not a

negative fusion result. PHM was already under way. REASSEMBLE subsequently provided

native completed-action outcomes, aligned observations and recording identities for

comparative evaluation; unresolved waveform timing and spoken outcome leakage prevented

an acoustic predictor.

       The September PADERBORN validation is retained as a bounded investigation, not

an additional principal case study. Severity was fixed per bearing. The requested

condition-only comparison favored the vibration predictor over the constant, whereas the

stricter bearing-and-condition contrast was inconclusive: MAE difference −.1184 [95% interval

−.2978, .0589]. The report promoted the stricter protocol as primary without resolving that

protocol fork. Recoverable bearing identity makes a shortcut plausible, not proven as the sole

mechanism. Gate 3 distillation was not run; withholding current as privileged information was

a study choice, not absence of current from the dataset.



G.3       Follow-Ups, Human Review and External Cases
September follow-ups examined longer sensor training, a same-sensor hybrid, anchored

adaptive coeﬀicients, matched augmentation and boundary-aware visual pooling. These

include positive exploratory results, but they did not execute a combined system containing

every favorable choice. The discontinued temporal-model work is distinct from completed

pooling experiments.

       The October experiments examined a spatial hybrid encoder, fine-tuning with unequal

training budgets, a matched two-epoch comparison and noise/quality interactions on selected

folds. GPU availability limited the 100-epoch comparison to one previously inspected

assessment fold. The later F2 fit also used a different set of training predictions from the

original experiment. The results therefore describe a follow-up on that fold.

       A reviewer used the September interface to assess 64 REASSEMBLE clips. The

returned ratings identified disagreements and potential boundary or presentation problems.

Adjudication was incomplete, and the evaluation used the original labels.

       The earlier “Milling Dataset” workspace and MSM refer to the same source, not



                                              250
Appendix G. Research Chronology


independent datasets. The 114-case feasibility pilot failed positive-group support; later

eligibility and alignment work admitted 1,895 intervals and enabled comparisons under several

grouping and label-2 policies. Thus, a failed pilot gate did not establish failure of the later

study. HANDOVER’s pre-score common-window amendment retained all 151 oﬀicial test

trials, but absent participant mapping prevented the mandated OOF bank for learned fusion.

These stopping and continuation decisions concern different requirements.

       REBOOT remained a feasibility investigation. The 28 October decision admitted no

normal/abnormal windows or supervised groups under the requested protocol despite 60

annotated cylinder-install episodes. Visual review was pending; no CNN extraction, training

or performance estimate followed. The wider screening excluded RoboFAC for lack of real

numerical counterparts, retained FAILURE only as a proposed RGB–audio pilot addressing a

different input question, and removed CRIA at the researcher’s request. Screening outcomes

are not model results.

       ViFailback progressed from cohort and telemetry checks to original frozen evaluation,

matched frozen/donor representation studies and bounded target-CNN adaptation. The 24

November closure records 79 of 111 planned fits complete. FULL20 has one seed and one

outer fold with all three required inner fits; FULL100 is a separate pilot. Their paired

adaptation effects remain inconclusive. The researcher stopped the remaining 32 FULL20 fits,

and corruption evaluation remained unexecuted. This revision extends reporting through that

closure while preserving the earlier protocols and complete frozen/donor results.




                                               251
Appendix H

HANDOVER Supplementary Case
Study


H.1       Objective and Relationship to the Research

          Questions
HANDOVER tests whether paired video and force–torque signals can identify the recorded

outcome of a completed human–robot handover. The single-input predictors address

representation choice (SQ1), and averaging their probabilities tests the contribution of the

second input on the same trials (SQ2). For SQ3, controlled blur, sensor noise, and

single-stream fallback show how the baselines behave when input quality or availability

changes. For SQ4, class-specific decisions at a fixed threshold complement the ranking scores.

Missing participant identifiers limit the uncertainty analysis and prevent participant-disjoint

fitting of the proposed learned fusion model.

       The HANDOVER dataset paper compared video, force/torque and gripper inputs for

completed-trial failure detection (Thoduka et al., 2024b). Its five-class I3D model achieved

69.1% accuracy with video and force/torque, falling to 67.9% when the gripper input was

added. The authors also investigated temporal action modelling with auxiliary action

supervision. The binary experiment here evaluates a different target and uses AP, which

precludes a numerical comparison with those five-class accuracies.




                                                252
Appendix H. HANDOVER Supplementary Case Study


H.2       Dataset and Common-Support Cohort
The source describes successful and failed robot handovers (Thoduka et al., 2024b). The

evaluation uses the pinned Zenodo v1 release (Thoduka et al., 2024a), with 589 native trials

involving Toyota HSR and Kinova Gen3 and 17 participants reported by its datasheet. Native

outcomes are success, no approach, outcome 2, and drop. Outcome 2 means no grasp for

robot-to-human handover or no release for human-to-robot handover. The binary target maps

success to zero and every nonzero native outcome to failure. Trial IDs restart across oﬀicial

splits, so identities are split-qualified; an ID is not a participant surrogate.

        The native oﬀicial split contains 337 training, 101 validation and 151 test trials. The

common-support cohort retains 587: 335 train, 101 validation and all 151 test trials, after two

training trials fail the timing gate. The test has 41 successes and 110 failures, giving positive

prevalence 0.728477. Released outcome counts govern the analysis where a small datasheet

discrepancy changes the distribution of outcome 2 versus drop; it does not change the binary

failure total. The oﬀicial participant-based split is the publisher’s claim, not an independently

verified identity separation.



H.3       Preparation and Input Construction
The initial full-video analysis excluded 92 test trials because their final frames extended

beyond wrench coverage. Before scoring the models, the observation window was defined as

the intersection of camera and wrench coverage for every trial, class and robot. The 16

source-clock bin-centre frames and numerical statistics use that same window. All oﬀicial test

trials could then be assessed. The completed-trial label can still depend on an event after the

end of the shared window.

        For the authors’ resampling procedure, matching the nearest raw wrench timestamp

reproduced the supplied arrays under a maximum permitted gap of 0.1 s. Video presentation

timestamps and sensor time refer to separate clocks. Six statistics for each of six force/torque

channels give 36 numerical features. The HSR measures wrench directly, whereas the Gen3

estimates it from joint torques. Fourteen traces met the timing criterion but were zero on

every channel. These trials were included with their original labels.




                                                253
Appendix H. HANDOVER Supplementary Case Study


H.4       Models and Fitting Procedure
The pretrained visual condition (P) used the frozen ResNet18-vd CNN backbone inside the

RT-DETR-R18vd checkpoint. Spatial and temporal means produced one 896-dimensional

vector per trial. A newly fitted 896–128–1 head trained for ten fixed epochs. Averaging the

frames removed their order. The numerical branch fitted training-only scaling and balanced

L2 logistic regression to the 36 statistics. A fixed HGB control, a training-prior predictor, and

equal averaging of the visual and logistic predictions completed the five baselines. Each was

evaluated under three fixed seeds. Learned stacking and the quality gates required participant

identifiers for their training predictions.

        HANDOVER uses statistical wrench descriptors in place of the REASSEMBLE

raw-sequence temporal branch. Its own data determine the visual head and sensor scaler.

None of the fitted source-classifier components or F2 coeﬀicients were transferred. The

planned R condition required a REASSEMBLE-trained CNN checkpoint that met the

HANDOVER selection criteria. No eligible checkpoint was established for that condition.



H.5       Evaluation and Results
The primary metric is failure-positive AP on the same 151 oﬀicial test trials, computed

separately for each seed. Table H.1 presents the three-seed mean and descriptive spread. The

deterministic numerical branches repeat identical predictions across seed labels; their zero

spread does not imply independent replication.

Table H.1
HANDOVER Test Performance on the Common Set of Trials

                        Model                       Mean AP Seed SD
                        Training-prior predictor 0.728477 0.000000
                        Visual                       0.771647 0.006411
                        Sensor logistic              0.823624 0.000000
                        Sensor HGB                   0.906319 0.000000
                        Averaging                    0.803102 0.013458
Note. Failure is the positive class. All models use the same 151 trials.


        Averaging increased AP by 0.031455 relative to vision and reduced it by 0.020522

relative to logistic sensing. All three seed differences against sensing were negative.

Failure-class F1 also reflects the high proportion of failures. With a threshold of 0.5 the prior

                                               254
Appendix H. HANDOVER Supplementary Case Study


predictor calls every trial a failure, giving F1 of 0.842912 and zero specificity for success.

Logistic sensing had higher AP than vision but a success specificity of only 0.170732.

       Under the strongest blur, averaging yielded AP of 0.779066, compared with 0.823624

for intact logistic sensing. At the highest sensor-noise level, averaging yielded 0.796955, noisy

logistic sensing 0.824952 and HGB 0.731344. Removing either input entirely triggered a fixed

fallback to the surviving branch.



H.6       Participant Identity and the Learned-Fusion

          Restriction
F2, F2-aug, QG-clean and QG-aug required out-of-fold branch probabilities from

participant-disjoint splits. The mapping from trial to participant was unavailable. Trial IDs,

robot IDs and task labels do not identify individual participants. The oﬀicial validation set

retained its original role, and none of these four fusion methods was fitted.

       The missing participant mapping also prevents estimation of a participant-cluster

interval. A trial bootstrap would treat repeated observations from the same person as

independent. The variation across seeds describes optimization on a fixed set of test trials.

Participant independence is reported for the oﬀicial split by the dataset authors, but could not

be checked from trial-level identities.



H.7       Interpretation of the HANDOVER Results
On the 151 handovers, logistic sensing ranked failures better than the visual model or simple

averaging. The HGB sensor model achieved the highest clean AP among the five baselines.

These results concern predictions made from the common camera and wrench observation

window.

       Whether a learned fusion rule could improve on sensing remains open for this cohort.

The proposed comparison requires a participant mapping or equivalent grouping information

to obtain independent training predictions for the fusion model. Without that comparison the

study provides no estimate of an effect from learned reliability weighting or transfer of

source-trained CNN weights.

I.1     Implementation and Experimentation
This example reconstructs part of the preserved implementation request. It records the

intended work. The configurations and run results document which experiments were

completed.

can you check what is already there in the project first?
go through the code and the previous results before changing anything.
i want to compare the individual models and the fusion methods.
check the datasets and what the labels actually mean.
check if the video and sensor inputs belong to the same sample.
make sure samples from the same group dont end up on both sides of the split.
keep the experiment settings fixed before checking the test results.
use the same evaluation setup for the models being compared in each study.
check how the predictions for training the fusion model are generated.
keep the assessment samples separate from training and model selection.
run the planned experiments only if the required checks pass.
keep the existing results and save the new work separately.
save the configs, seeds, predictions and the training details.
if something fails, keep the failed attempt and explain what happened.
also check the planned missing or degraded modality conditions.
report when fusion helps, when it does not, and when the result is unclear.
dont change the experiment just to get a better score.
keep the folders clear so i can follow what was done later.

I.2     Fetching and Checking Results
This example reconstructs the request to retrieve and check the experimental results and

assess options for local access. The related GitHub discussion considered transfer of the files.

The historical record documents no upload.

can you fetch the saved results from the experiments?
i need the csv files, figures, matrices and the technical results for my thesis.
first check which files are actually available.
keep the results connected to the correct experiment and configuration.
check the sample ids and labels before comparing the predictions.
check the reported numbers from the saved predictions where possible.
keep results from different tasks and datasets clearly identified.
explain what the individual models and fusion methods actually did.
show the comparison tables and figures that support the conclusions.
include the failures and the results where there was no clear improvement.
mention if a result is incomplete or if the required file is missing.
explain the limitations in simple words so i can use them in the thesis.
also check what i would need to keep these results on my laptop.
check which files can go to github and what needs a separate download.
dont call the upload complete before checking the required files.




I.3     Archiving and Version Control of Experimental

        Outputs
The author used AI assistance to plan how to archive experimental outputs from the

university server locally and track them with Git. The discussion covered CSV summaries,

figures, and matrices and considered GitHub as an option for repository hosting. The

available records document this planning only.



I.4     Provenance
The two reconstructions draw on the preserved implementation and audit requests. The

original implementation chats were unavailable, so the precise wording and authorship of

individual lines cannot be verified. Section I.3 summarises the subsequent discussion about

access to experimental outputs. Proposed prompts for future work fall outside this record.

I.5     Targeted Scientific Revision
The 25 November request approved sixteen proposed revisions, then required stronger positive

architectural justification, an explicit literature comparison in Chapter 9, dataset-specific

interpretation, retained effect sizes and uncertainty, the complete relevant investigation

history, and the November 24 ViFailback update. It authorized application to a preserved

working copy, a traceable diff and compilation, while retaining the original chapter structure,

questions and registered title. It prohibited new training and restarting literature discovery.

This paragraph summarizes the request; it is not a verbatim transcript or an independent

validation of the resulting text.
